Optimal Sequential Recommendations: Exploiting User and Item Structure
Mina Karzand, Guy Bresler

Abstract
We consider an online model for recommendation systems, with each user being recommended an item at each time-step and providing ’like’ or ’dislike’ feedback. A latent variable model specifies the user preferences: both users and items are clustered into types. The model captures structure in both the item and user spaces, as used by item-item and user-user collaborative filtering algorithms. We study the situation in which the type preference matrix has i.i.d. entries. Our main contribution is an algorithm that simultaneously uses both item and user structures, proved to be near-optimal via corresponding information-theoretic lower bounds. In particular, our analysis highlights the sub-optimality of using only one of item or user structure (as is done in most collaborative filtering algorithms).



1
Introduction

The music we listen to, the movies we watch, and the products we buy: more often than not, they are recommended to us by algorithms. Given the importance of these recommendation algorithms, it makes sense to try to design optimal ones. A basic criterion for optimality, that captures the first-order experience of users in a recommendation system, is to maximize the proportion of recommendations that are liked,
1
1

1

A common strategy for developing recommendation algorithms is to frame it as a matrix completion task. This entails recovering unobserved matrix entries given a subset of observed ones, often assuming low-rank. However, optimizing for accuracy of the entire estimated matrix ignores the user experience: A highly accurate estimate of bad recommendations provides no benefit to the user.

similar to
[
11
,
23
]

The goal of this paper is to gain insight into the design of recommendation algorithms by finding a statistically optimal algorithm within the context of a natural model for recommendation systems. One of our findings is that the best way to obtain information about users and items in order to make good recommendations depends on the time horizon and its relation to various system parameters including the number of users, the diversity of users, and richness of the items; there are a number of operating regimes depending on these parameters.
It goes without saying that the nature of any insight obtained is intertwined with the choice of model. We use the same model as
[
11
]
, closely related to those studied in
[
10
,
12
]
. The model is different from those in other papers on the topic; we now motivate its key features.

1.1
Our Recommendation System Model

Recommendation systems are inherently dynamic. Each recommendation gives an opportunity to observe user behavior and thus influences the system’s understanding of user preferences and item characteristics, thereby shaping the potential effectiveness of subsequent recommendations. This creates a fundamental challenge: balancing the need to explore to obtain new information with the desire to leverage existing knowledge to provide high-quality recommendations. This exploration-exploitation dilemma is a central theme in the study of multi-armed bandit (MAB) and related problems (e.g.,
[
13
,
28
,
36
]
). A key difference exists, however; whereas MAB algorithms ultimately converge to a single, repeated action, users would generally find repeated recommendations of the same item undesirable. To address this, we impose the constraint that an item can be recommended to a given user at most once, as done previously in
[
10
,
12
,
3
,
23
]
.

Our recommendation system model has a fixed set of users, each of whom is recommended an item at each time-step. The system then receives binary feedback, ‘like’ or ‘dislike’, from each of the users.
The user preferences are described by a latent variable model in which each user is associated with a user type and each item is associated with an item type.
Users of the same type have identical preferences for all of the items and items of the same type result in identical feedback when recommended to any given user.
2
2

2

A similar model of data to ours, in which there is an underlying clustering of rows and columns, has been studied in other settings
[
38
,
44
]
.
This model for user preferences has been motivated empirically in
[
10
]
and captures structure amongst both users and items.
The measure of performance is expected regret, equal to the expected number of bad recommendations made per user over a time horizon of interest. The precise formulation of our model is given in Section
2
.

In this model, information about users and items is only obtained via user feedback. In particular, there is no feature data on the users or items (such as age, location, and gender of users or genre, actors, and director of movies) as used by content filtering algorithms; this allows us to better focus on the dynamics driven by information gain over time.

Broadly, algorithms based on observed user preferences are called collaborative filtering (CF)
[
21
]

and are used by virtually all industrial recommendation systems. There are two main variants: User-user CF, where recommendation to a user is done by finding similar users and recommending items liked by these users; and item-item CF, where items similar to those liked by the user are found and then recommended.
Versions of item-item
[
11
,
12
]
and user-user
[
11
,
10
,
23
]
CF were analyzed previously.
The papers
[
10
,
12
]
did not prove lower bounds and hence could not make any claims regarding optimality. Within the same model as we study here,
[
11
]
proved information-theoretic lower bounds showing item-item or user-user CF to be optimal in certain extreme parameter regimes of the model, with structure only in the user space or only in the item space.
The present paper goes significantly beyond that work by addressing the general situation with nontrivial structure in both item and user space.

1.2
Our Contributions

There are two main contributions of this paper: (1) We prove a novel multi-part information-theoretic lower bound on the regret suffered by any algorithm; (2)
We propose a new and essentially optimal algorithm that uses both user and item structure in a novel way. The performance guarantee of our algorithm matches our lower bound to within a logarithmic factor.
Our paper is the first to characterize optimal regret in a model that has both user and item structure, and does so across the entire spectrum of relative richness of item space versus user space.

Characterizing the best possible performance in a simple model of recommendation systems yields a variety of engineering insights. As described in Section
3
, there are five different operating regimes for the regret curve in our main result. In each of these there is a different optimal pathway for obtaining information about preferences of users for items. Our algorithm makes use of the optimal pathway in each regime, while our lower bound shows optimality by carefully accounting for the various possible ways of obtaining information.

Our lower bounds are, to the best of our knowledge, the first lower bounds for a natural model of recommendations in the online setting that correctly capture dependence on the item and user structure.
Our lower bound is obtained by
arguing that reduced uncertainty about the preference of a user for an item can only be achieved via highly informative and necessarily uncertain recommendations.
Due to there being both item and user structure, there are several natural ways to get information about the preference of a user for an item. Our lower bound is based on identifying complementary scenarios in which we show that there is insufficient information to have confidence that a given recommendation will be liked, together with showing constraints on the number of occurrences of these scenarios.

One insight from the analysis in
[
11
]
was that the item-item algorithm must limit the exploration to only a subset of the items types, where the size of this subset depends on the system parameters and time-horizon. It was noted that the straightforward approach to Item-Item CF algorithms is to learn the whole preference matrix, which results in a highly suboptimal cold-start time. This feature also exists in the current paper, where it is crucial to avoid exploring too much – instead, obtaining just enough information about the model leads to optimal regret.

Our system model, defined precisely in Section
2
, is quite simple, but already exhibits a complex and rich set of phenomena. While we believe that this model captures the most salient features of item and user structure, a natural next step is to generalize to more intricate item and user structures. In order to better focus on the latent information structures in the problem, feedback from users is noiseless, but it is straightforward to incorporate noise into our model. One might also wish to model arrival and departure of users or items, change of users over time, and so forth.
We hope that this paper can be used as a stepping stone towards the principled design of near-optimal recommendation systems in more complex scenarios.

1.3
Comparison to Related Work

We now make some comparisons between our work and several lines of work in the literature. There are four main points of distinction between our work and prior work, each of which is very different from ours in some subset of the following: (i) performance metric;
(ii) exploration due to online formulation;
(iii) optimal use of user and item structure;
(iv) no repetition constraint.

Traditional approaches to recommendation systems often focus on recommending new items by completing a partially observed user-item preference matrix. A core assumption is that a limited number of factors influence user preferences, implying low-rank structure in the preference matrix and allowing to draw on the large literature on low-rank matrix completion

[
42
,
35
,
14
,
40
,
16
,
26
,
6
,
15
,
25
]
. As noted in the introduction, this approach fails to address the online nature of the problem, in which there is an opportunity to explore in a targeted way, and also the specific loss function over the matrix fails to capture user experience of being recommended items.

Hazan et. al. in
[
22
]
make the observation that not all entries of a partially observed low rank matrix are equally difficult to estimate. Their goal is

partial matrix completion
: identifying a large subset of entries that can be completed with high accuracy.
This is conceptually related to the fact that our algorithm sometimes ignores a portion of the preference matrix due to high cost of exploration.
However,
[
22
]
is nevertheless in an offline setting and gives no insight towards how best to explore, which is a focus of our work.
They do also consider a semi-online setting in which at each time-step an entry is revealed adversarially,
and the algorithm proposes a partial completion; it suffers regret based on the worst-possible matrix consistent with the revealed entries. As before, because the task is purely prediction-based without a choice of revealed entry, there is no exploration involved.

Several works carry out online matrix completion using iterative gradient based methods
[
24
,
30
,
41
,
20
]
. In these works, the objective is to continually achieve more accurate estimations of all entries of the preference matrix. This is in contrast to the performance measure in our work, which entails making as many good recommendations as possible in any given time horizon. One key observation that emerges from the analysis of our proposed optimal algorithm is that these two objectives can be contradictory in small time horizons: It is beneficial to intentionally avoid learning some part of the preference matrix completely. One other distinction from those works, as in the prior paragraph, is that the entries are revealed either adversarially or uniformly at random, so there is no exploration.

In multi armed bandit (MAB) modeling of recommendation systems, each user or user cluster is modeled as an agent and each item corresponds to an arm, as exemplified by
[
33
,
4
,
37
,
34
]
. The users receive distinct, possibly correlated expected rewards from each arm. The purpose of exploration is identifying the arm with the highest expected reward for each user, implying that the best arm (item) can be used (recommended) many times after successful identification. Their results quantify the number of samples needed to identify the best arm in terms of the ‘gap’ in the expected reward of the best arm from the suboptimal ones, number of users, etc. In contrast, we impose the constraint that each item can only be recommended at most once to each user, which dramatically reduces the benefit of finding a good item and also exploration must be continually done in order to find new items to recommend. This turns out to completely change the manner in which exploration must be carried out at all time horizons and leads to entirely new optimal algorithms.

The paper
[
3
]
considers a setup similar to ours, but slightly more general, with probabilities in the preference matrix. This captures inhomogeneity in the quality of items. Their results exhibit dependence on certain gaps in these probabilities, as is typical of MAB results, but fail to capture any nontrivial dependence on the structure in the item and user space, in any parameter regime. In contrast, our focus is on the impact of structure among items and users.

A few papers including
[
10
,
12
,
19
,
23
]
have analyzed online collaborative filtering. The papers
[
7
,
10
,
23
,
11
]
analyze a user-user CF algorithm and
[
12
,
11
]
analyzes an item-item CF algorithm in a slightly more flexible model with structured noise.
All of these papers only consider either user or item structure, but not both simultaneously.
Relative to these, our main contribution is in proposing an algorithm that makes use of structure in both user and item space—and in addition, obtaining nearly tight lower bounds in almost all parameter regimes.

Dabeer and coauthors
[
19
,
5
,
1
]
also use a quite similar model to ours, but with noisy feedback from users.
They focus on the exploitation phase of an online recommendation system: Given arbitrary prior noisy ratings,
they aim to identify the highest probability liked item for each user. They show approximate optimality of their algorithm, for their performance criterion, in various regimes of noise parameter.
In doing so, their objective and regime of interest ignore the benefit of further exploration and, as a result, the algorithms do not attempt to recommend items which reveal information about the underlying model that will in turn be useful for future exploitations.

We remark that disallowing repetition in recommendations to a given user has been used before
[
3
,
23
,
31
,
19
]
. The authors in
[
31
]
propose interesting heuristic algorithms based on information directed sampling; however, they do not derive any bounds on regret.

Algorithms that exploit structure in both the user and item space have been studied before in
[
39
,
43
,
27
,
8
,
9
]
. For example,
[
39
,
8
,
9
]
consider a more flexible latent variable model and study
offline
matrix completion where a subset of the entries are revealed and the goal is to guess the remaining entries. As noted above, the offline problem formulation is very different from our online setting and it does not give insight into how best to explore.

We remark that there have been several exciting developments in offline matrix completion, including a model where the observed entries are not uniformly random (e.g., users are more likely to watch a movie when they have a premonition that they will like it)
[
2
]
. In future work, it may be interesting to combine elements of that model with ours.

1.4
Notation

For an integer
a
we write
[a]=\{1,\cdots,a\}
. For real-valued
x
let
(x)_{+}=\max\{x,0\}
.
\lfloor x\rfloor
denotes the greatest integer less than or equal to
x
and
\lceil x\rceil
denotes the smallest integer greater than or equal to
x
and
\big\lfloor x\big\rfloor_{+}=\max\{0,\lfloor x\rfloor\}
. All logarithms are to the base of 2. The set of natural numbers (positive integers) is denoted by
\mathbb{N}
. We note here that variables or parameters in Figure
2
have the same meaning throughout the paper, but any others may take different values in each section.
We define
\big\lfloor x\big\rfloor_{+}=\max\{0,\lfloor x\rfloor\}
.
Numerical constants (
c,c_{1},c_{2}
and so forth) may take different values in different theorem statements unless explicitly stated otherwise.

2
Model

2.1
Problem Setup and Performance Metrics

We consider
N
users, represented by the set
\{1,\dots,N\}
. At each time
t=1,2,3,\dots
, the algorithm recommends an item
a_{u,t}\in\mathbb{N}
to each user
u
and receives binary feedback
L_{u,a_{u,s}}\in\{+1,-1\}
(representing ‘like’ or ‘dislike’, respectively). Following our earlier constraints, each item is recommended at most once to each user. We assume there is an infinite set of items, identified by the natural numbers, ensuring the algorithm can always make recommendations.

The history of interactions, denoted as

\mathcal{H}_{t}
, is defined as the set of all actions and feedback up to time
t
:

\mathcal{H}_{t}=\{a_{u,s},L_{u,a_{u,s}},\text{ for }u\in[N],s\in[t]\}
. We are interested in online collaborative filtering algorithms. In these algorithms, the action
a_{u,t}
is determined by a (possibly random) function of the history from the previous time step
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
. This randomness is encoded by a random variable
\zeta_{u,t}
, which is independent of all other variables. Thus, we have
a_{u,t}=f_{u,t}(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\zeta_{u,t})
for a deterministic function
f_{u,t}
.

Algorithm performance is evaluated after an arbitrary number of time-steps
T
. The performance metric is expected regret (simply called regret), defined as the expected number of disliked recommended items per user:

\mathtt{regret}(T)=\mathbb{E}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\mathds{1}[L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1]\,,

(1)

where the expectation is with respect to the randomness in the model and the algorithm.
Our algorithm uses knowledge of the time-horizon
T
. However, using a standard doubling trick (discussed in Appendix
I
) makes it possible to convert the proposed algorithm to one achieving the same (up to constant multiplicative factors) regret without this knowledge (see
[
17
,
29
]
for an example).
This alternative, where the algorithm is agnostic to
T
and needs to perform well over any time interval, is called
anytime regret
in the literature.

Aside from regret, the time at which recommendations become nontrivial is a key performance consideration, as users initially receive little value for their interaction with the system. The notorious “cold start” problem in recommendation systems arises from initial scarcity of information. To formalize this, we define the
cold start time
as the first point at which the slope of the regret curve with respect to
T
falls below the threshold
\gamma/\log(NT)
:

\textsf{coldstart}(\gamma)=\min\Big\{T:\frac{\mathtt{regret}(T)}{T}\leq\frac{\gamma}{\log(NT)}\Big\}\,.

Equivalently, this is also the first time that the expected number of bad recommendations is substantially sublinear in the total number of recommendations:

\displaystyle\textsf{coldstart}(\gamma)=\min\Big\{T:N\mathtt{regret}(T)\leq\gamma\frac{NT}{\log(NT)}\Big\}\,.

(2)

Requiring sublinearity is stricter than the definition of cold start time in
[
12
]
and
[
11
]
, where cold-start is defined as the first stime the slope of regret is bounded by constant
\gamma
.

2.2
User Preferences

We study a latent-variable model for the preferences
L_{u,i}\in\{+1,-1\}
of the users for the items, based on the idea that there are relatively few
types of users
and/or few
types of items
.
Each user
u\in[N]
has a user type
\tau_{U}(u)
i.i.d. uniform on
[\texttt{q}_{U}]=\{1,\dots,\texttt{q}_{U}\}
, where
\texttt{q}_{U}
is the number of user types.
We assume for ease of presentation of the results that

20\texttt{q}_{U}\log^{2}\texttt{q}_{U}<N
. In this regime there is nontrivial structure in the user space: there are at least
0.5\cdot N/\texttt{q}_{U}
users of each type with high probability.
In the complementary regime
\texttt{q}_{U}\gg N
most users have their own type and the analysis reduces to replacing
\texttt{q}_{U}
by
N
.
3
3

3

The remaining (narrow) range
\texttt{q}_{U}<N<\texttt{q}_{U}\log^{2}\texttt{q}_{U}
is addressed in our earlier work
[
11
]
.

Similarly, each item
i\in\mathbb{N}
has a random item type
\tau_{I}(i)
i.i.d. uniform on
[\texttt{q}_{I}]
, where
\texttt{q}_{I}
is the number of item types.The random variables
\{\tau_{U}(u)\}_{1\leq u\leq N}
and
\{\tau_{I}(i)\}_{1\leq i}
are assumed to be jointly independent.
We are interested in understanding the system behavior as a function of
\texttt{q}_{U}
and
\texttt{q}_{I}
, which parameterize the complexity of the user preferences. It turns out that somewhat degenerate behavior emerges when these are too small, so
throughout the paper we assume that both
\texttt{q}_{I}
and
\texttt{q}_{U}
are at least
100\log N
. The factor of 100 is merely there to simplify the mathematical arguments.
We will also assume
N>100
.

for item type

for item

at time

Notation for the System Model

\tau_{U}(u)\sim\mathsf{unif}([\texttt{q}_{U}])
i.i.d.

\tau_{I}(i)\sim\mathsf{unif}([\texttt{q}_{I}])
i.i.d.

\xi_{w,\textit{j}}\sim\mathsf{unif}(\{-1,+1\})
i.i.d.

Figure 2:
Model Assumptions

All users of a given type have identical preferences for all the items, and similarly all items of a given type are rated in the same way by any particular user. The entire collection of user preferences
(L_{u,i})_{u,i}
is therefore encoded into a much smaller
preference matrix

\Xi=(\xi_{w,\textit{j}})\in\{-1,+1\}^{\texttt{q}_{U}\times\texttt{q}_{I}}
, which specifies the preference of each user type for each item type. The preference
L_{u,i}
of user
u\in[N]
for item
i\in\mathbb{N}
is the preference
\xi_{\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i)}
of the associated user type
\tau_{U}(u)
for the item type
\tau_{I}(i)
in the matrix
\Xi
,
i.e.
,

L_{u,i}=\xi_{\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i)}\,.

We assume that the entries of
\Xi
are i.i.d.,
\xi_{w,\textit{j}}=+1
w.p.
1/2
and
\xi_{w,\textit{j}}=-1
w.p.
1/2
. Generalizing our results to i.i.d. entries with bias
p
is straightforward. However, the independence assumption among entries of
\Xi
is quite strong and an important future research direction is to obtain results for more realistic preference matrices.

A modest generalization of this model is to have noisy feedback with
L_{u,i}=\xi_{\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i)}\cdot z_{u,i}
, where
z_{u,i}
are i.i.d. with
\mathbb{P}[z_{u,i}=+1]=1-\gamma
and
\mathbb{P}[z_{u,i}=-1]=\gamma
.
In
[
11
]
,
the proposed user-user algorithm is modified to handle noisy feedback of this form. However, since the challenge of dealing with noise is a well-studied theme in online learning, and also technically straightforward to handle in our setting, we focus here on the latent-variable structure of users and items and refrain from studying the effect of noise.

3
Main result

In this section we state our main result, which gives an optimal curve for regret in our recommendation system model. For the various regimes and quantities to be interpretable, we first discuss some conceptual items.

3.1
Conceptual Preliminaries

3.1.1
Comparison of items and users

The basic way that information can be extracted from the system is via
comparison
of two items or two users. Two items
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are understood to be
similar
if a subset of users
\mathcal{U}
of appropriate size
\log(\texttt{q}_{I})
rates them identically, i.e.
L_{u,i_{1}}=L_{u,i_{2}}
for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Analogously, two users
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are understood to be similar if they rate a random subset of items
\mathcal{I}
of size
\log(\texttt{q}_{U})
identically, i.e.

L_{u_{1},i}=L_{u_{2},i}
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
.

Note that there is an inherent asymmetry between users and items: multiple users can rate a given item simultaneously, but each user rates one and only one item at each time step. Thus, similarity of two items can be determined in as few as two time-steps, while determining similarity between two users requires
\log(\texttt{q}_{U})
time-steps.

3.1.2
Clustering of Items and Users

The users and items have types, and any given algorithm will at some point have enough information to cluster the users or (some of) the items. The operating regimes are in part determined by the extent to which items and/or users ought to be clustered within the given time horizon. The nuance here is that even if it is
possible
to cluster users or items, there is a regret cost that must be incurred to obtain the needed information, and this must be weighed against the benefit. In Section
5
we carry out a careful heuristic analysis of the cost versus benefit of obtaining different types of information that explains the regret curve and the various operating regimes.

3.1.3
Partial Learning of Preference Matrix

Aside from item and user types, the basic uncertainty faced by a recommendation algorithm consists of the preference matrix encoding whether each user type likes or dislikes each item type. The basic fact that all items from a liked type can be recommended to a user, because by definition likes all of them, implies that for small time horizons optimal algorithms avoid exploring the entire preference matrix. This phenomenon is responsible for the specific shape of the regret curve (and enters into the heuristic derivation in Section
5
).

3.2
Theorem Statement

We next state our main result. We will momentarily interpret it, but for now the main features of the result are: (i) there are five regimes and the regret behaves differently in each; (ii) this regret curve is achievable by the algorithm we propose; (iii) this regret curve is optimal, in that no algorithm can beat it. Thus, the different operating regimes and transition points are inherent to the recommendation system problem.

Our algorithm,
RecommendationSystem
, is described at a high level in Section
5
together with heuristic analysis.
Pseudocode is given in Section
6
.

Theorem 3.1

(Main Result)
.

Under the modeling assumptions in Figure
2
,
there are universal constants
c
and
C
such that the following holds. For any time horizon
T
, algorithm
RecommendationSystem
achieves
N\mathtt{regret}(T)\leq C\,N\mathtt{R}(T)\log^{3/2}(N\mathtt{R}(T))
, where
\mathtt{R}(T)
is defined below. Conversely, if
\texttt{q}_{U}>(\log\texttt{q}_{I})^{2}
and
\texttt{q}_{I}>(\log N)^{5}
then any algorithm must incur
N\mathtt{regret}(T)\geq c\,\frac{N\mathtt{R}(T)}{\log(N\mathtt{R}(T))}
. Thus, the optimal total regret is

N\mathtt{regret}(T)=\widetilde{\Theta}(N\mathtt{R}(T))\,.

The function
\mathtt{R}(T)

is defined in a piece-wise manner in the table below, with the support of each piece given in terms of the following functions of system parameters:

\displaystyle T_{1}=\log\texttt{q}_{U},\quad T_{2}=\texttt{q}_{I}/N,\quad T_{3}=\texttt{q}_{I}/\texttt{q}_{U},\quad T_{4}=N(\log\texttt{q}_{U})^{2}/\texttt{q}_{I},\quad T_{5}=\texttt{q}_{I}\texttt{q}_{U}\,.

and

and

and
\texttt{q}_{U}<\log\texttt{q}_{I}

(a)

\mathtt{R}(T)
when
T_{1}<T_{2}
and
\log\texttt{q}_{I}\leq\texttt{q}_{U}
.

(b)

\mathtt{R}(T)
when
T_{2}\leq T_{q}
and
\texttt{q}_{U}<\log\texttt{q}_{I}

Figure 3:
Two possible regret curves
\mathtt{R}(T)
and their various operation regimes. The piece-wise curves are scaled by the appropriate constant factor so that regret is continuous.

This theorem is proved in Appendix
F
by combining the lower bound on regret in Theorem
4.12
and the upper bound in Theorem
6.4
.

Corollary 3.2

(Cold-Start)
.

Under the modeling assumptions in Figure
2
,
and assuming
\texttt{q}_{U}>(\log\texttt{q}_{I})^{2}
and
\texttt{q}_{I}>(\log N)^{5}
,
there are universal constants
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}>0
such that the following hold:

1.

The cold start time of
RecommendationSystem
is upper bounded as

\textsf{coldstart}(\gamma)\leq\min\Big\{\log^{2}N,\max\big\{\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}\log\texttt{q}_{I}}{N}\log^{2}(N\texttt{q}_{I}),\,16\big\}\Big\}\,.

2.

Also, the cold start time of any algorithm is lower bounded as

\textsf{coldstart}(\gamma^{\prime})\geq\min\Big\{\log N\,\log\texttt{q}_{U},\max\big\{\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}\log^{2}\texttt{q}_{I}}{N},\,16\big\}\Big\}\,.

Thus, the cold start time of our algorithm is essentially optimal up to logarithmic factors. We remark that the maximum with constant 16 is needed, because even as
N\to\infty
the cold start time does not tend to zero—it is always at least one.
The proof appears in Section
G
.

3.3
Discussion of Main Result

The regret curve in Theorem
3.1
is fairly complex, with its various regimes that may appear in different orders or not at all. However, each transition point and portion of regret curve has a simple explanation that gives insight into the basic features of optimal recommendation algorithms. As mentioned above, Section
5
gives a full explanation of the regret curve. For now, we limit ourselves to some remarks about the implications of Theorem
3.1
:

•

For time-horizons in
\mathtt{T}_{\mathsf{Item}}
,
\mathtt{T}_{\mathsf{User}}
, and
\mathtt{T}_{\mathsf{Asymptotic}}
, it is optimal to use only one of item structure or user structure.

•

For time-horizons in
\mathtt{T}_{\mathsf{Hybrid}}
, both item and user structures are used. The cost of learning the preference for an item is shared by all users in a given cluster, and additional items of the same type as a liked item are then also recommended.

•

T_{1}
is the cold-start time (up to multiplicative logarithmic factors) for a pure user-user collaborative filtering algorithm: on the order of
\log\texttt{q}_{U}
ratings of the same item are both necessary and sufficient to compare two users.

•

T_{2}
is the cold-start time (up to multiplicative logarithmic factors) of a pure item-item collaborative filtering algorithm: on the order of
\texttt{q}_{I}
items must be seen in order to have two items of the same type, and this effort is divided by the
N
users.

•

The condition
T_{1}<T_{2}
corresponds to the regime when the cold-start of an (optimal) user-user algorithm is shorter than the cold-start time of an (optimal) item-item algorithm. An implication of our theorem is that the minimum of these two cold-start times is best-possible.

•

If
T_{1}<T_{2}
and
\log\texttt{q}_{I}\leq\texttt{q}_{U}
, for time horizons larger than
T_{3}
, using both item and user structure improves on only using user structure.

•

The significance of
T_{4}
is that an optimal item-item comparison algorithm will at that point have recommended enough items to the users that the user clusters become evident, at which point there is no additional cost to using both user and item structure.

•

For time horizons in
\mathtt{T}_{\mathsf{Asymptotic}}
, an optimal algorithm will have learned the user types, a clustering over a set of items, and the preference matrix encoding the preferences of users for item types.

However, due to the constraint that recommendations to a given user cannot be repeated, new items must be introduced. Discovering the types of these new items (i.e., how they relate to already-seen items) incurs regret. Alternatively, the preference for the new items can be learned via similarity between the users, completely ignoring item structure. For large time horizons in
\mathtt{T}_{\mathsf{Asymptotic}}
, the optimal algorithm chooses the better of the two options (decided by comparison of
\log\texttt{q}_{I}
and
\texttt{q}_{U}
), rather than both at once.

•

After the cold-start time, the function
\mathtt{R}(T)
is the sum of a part that does not depend on
T
and a part that does. The latter determines the rate of increase of regret per unit of time. This rate is nonincreasing in
T
. The former is the cost of reaching each regime. For example, roughly speaking
\log\texttt{q}_{U}
recommendations to each user is necessary to reach the Hybrid regime. This interpretation will be expanded in Section
5.2
.

•

One surprising consequence of the various regimes is that if for large time horizons in
\mathtt{T}_{\mathsf{Asymptotic}}
user-user CF is better than item-item CF, then for
all time horizons
it is not helpful to use both item and user structure together.

4
Information-theoretic Lower Bound on Regret

Theorem
3.1
constitutes a performance guarantee for our algorithm as well as an information-theoretic lower bound which holds for any algorithm and shows optimality of the one we propose. While both results require new ideas, we feel that the conceptual novelty leading to the lower bound is more significant. For this reason we start with the lower bound.

In this section we describe the main components of the argument.
We remark that the lower bound implicit in Theorem
3.1
is a slightly looser version of the precise bound that we obtain, which is stated as Theorem
4.12
.

High-level Strategy

There are three high-level components to the argument:

1.

Identifying
bad recommendation events
, in which some key portion of information is unavailable, and showing that they induce regret (Section
4.1
).

2.

Deriving various lower bounds on the number of bad events in terms of system trajectories (Section
4.2
). This part is purely combinatorial and entails careful tracking of recommendations in terms of the type of information they can provide.

3.

Combining these bounds to get a lower bound on regret (Section
4.3
). Here we make crucial use of the fact that each item can be recommended to each user at most once, as well as proving facts about the empirical frequencies of item and user types.

4.1
Bad Recommendations and Associated Scenarios

Bad Recommendations

The history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
at time
t
denotes all feedback received from the users at times
1,\dots,t
.
To lower bound regret, we define a recommendation
a_{u,t}
to user
u
at time
t
to be a
bad
(or uncertain) recommendation when the posterior probability of the event
\{L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\}
given the history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
is close to (or smaller than) a constant, e.g.,
1/3
.
Note that this refers only to the
confidence
that the recommendation is liked given the history at the moment the recommendation is made: a bad recommendation is not always disliked.
A lower bound on regret follows directly from a lower bound on the expected number of bad recommendations (Lemma
4.3
). We next identify events leading to bad recommendations.

Bad Recommendation Events

We identify four scenarios in which recommending item
i
to user
u
at time
t
is necessarily bad, captured by events
\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t},\dots,\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}
defined precisely in Section
4.1.1
and proved to be bad in Proposition
4.2
:

1.

\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}
: If at time
t
item
i
and user
u
are associated to too few ratings, then the type of item
i
and the type of user
u
are both uncertain leading to uncertainty in the
location
of the relevant entry of the preference matrix, which has roughly half
+1
and half −1.

2.

\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}
: Even if the type of user
u
is known, as long as the type of item
i
is uncertain and item
i
has not been recommended to any user with the same type as
u
, then recommending
i
to
u
is a ‘bad’ recommendation.
The uncertainty is in the type of item
i
, which corresponds to choosing the entry in the row of the preference matrix for user
u
’s type, and again the typical row has roughly half each of
+1
and −1.

3.

\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}
: Even if the type of item
i
is known, as long as the type of user
u
is uncertain and no item with the same type as
i
has been recommended to
u
before, then recommending
i
to
u
is a ‘bad’ recommendation.
The uncertainty here is a symmetric situation to the prior one with the roles of user and item flipped.

4.

\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}
: Even if the types of item
i
and user
u
are both known,
if no user with the same type as
u
has seen an item with the same type as
i
, then
the actual value of the entry of the preference matrix corresponding to the types of item
i
and user
u
is uncertain.

Figure
4
illustrates bad recommendations of the various types.

Figure 4:
The green nodes on the left denote a subset of items and the blue nodes on the right denote a subset of users. Edges are between two items (or two users) that have been compared. Red edges between items and users denote previous recommendations.
There are bad recommendations of four types: (i)
i_{2}\longrightarrow u_{2}
is of type
\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}
; (ii)
i_{2}\longrightarrow u_{1}
is of type
\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}
; (iii)
i_{1}\longrightarrow u_{2}
is of type
\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}
; (iv)
i_{1}\longrightarrow u_{1}
is of type
\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}
.
i_{3}\longrightarrow u_{1}
is not necessarily a bad recommendation since
i_{3}
has been recommended to
u_{3}
before, which is presumed to be of the same type as
u_{1}
.

Remark 4.1
.

The first three categories of bad recommendations can occur even if the preference matrix is entirely known and are due to uncertainty in what is the relevant entry of this matrix. The last category is due to uncertainty in the value of an entry whose relevance may already be nearly certain.

4.1.1
Bad Recommendation Scenarios: Formal Definitions

Knowledge of types

We now introduce notation summarizing the feedback seen by the algorithm, i.e. the history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
, as turns out to be relevant for learning the type of a user or item.

1.

Let
c_{i}^{t}
be
the number of user types
which have rated item
i
(strictly) before time
t
, i.e.,

\displaystyle c_{i}^{t}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{v=1}^{\texttt{q}_{U}}\mathds{1}\big\{a_{u,s}=i\text{ for some }s<t\text{ and }u\text{ such that }\tau_{U}(u)=v\big\}\,.

(3)

Inspired by the intution in Section
3.1.1
,
our proxy for having nontrivial knowledge of the type of an item
i
is
c_{i}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I}

where
\mathsf{s}_{I}\approx\log\texttt{q}_{I}
and will be specified later in (
25
).

2.

Let
d_{u}^{t}
be
the number of item types
that were rated by user
u
strictly before time
t
, i.e.,

\displaystyle d_{u}^{t}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\textit{j}=1}^{\texttt{q}_{I}}\mathds{1}\big\{\tau_{I}(a_{u,s})=\textit{j}\text{ for some }s<t\big\}\,.

(4)

Inspired by the intution in Section
3.1.1
,
our proxy for having nontrivial knowledge of the type
of a user
u
is
d_{u}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{U}
where
\mathsf{s}_{U}\approx\log\texttt{q}_{U}
will be specified later in (
25
).

Note that
c_{i}^{t}
and
d_{u}^{t}
are nondecreasing in
t
.

Events capturing knowledge of preferences

We will need events (labeled
\mathcal{K}
for “knowledge”) capturing information available about the preference of user
u
for item
i
assuming that users have been clustered according to types, items have been clustered according to types, or both. These events are motivated by the
\mathcal{B}^{b}_{u,t}
scenarios for
b=2,3,4
described above.

1.

Preference of a user type for an item.

Let
\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}}
be the event that a user with type
\tau_{U}(u)
has rated item
i
by the end of time
t-1
, i.e.,

\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}}=\Big\{{\text{there exists }u^{\prime}\in[N]:a_{u^{\prime},s}=i}\text{ for some }s<t\text{ with }\tau_{U}(u)=\tau_{U}(u^{\prime})\Big\}\,.

(5)

2.

Preference of a user for an item type.

Let
\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})}
be the event that user
u
has rated an item of the same item type as item
i
by the end of time
t-1
, i.e.,

\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})}=\Big\{{\text{there exists }i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:a_{u,s}=i^{\prime}}\text{ for some }s<t\text{ with }\tau_{I}(i)=\tau_{I}(i^{\prime})\Big\}\,.

(6)

3.

Preference of a user type for an item type.

Let
\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})}
be the event that a user with type
\tau_{U}(u)
has rated an item with type
\tau_{I}(i)
by the end of time
t-1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})}=\Big\{

\displaystyle{\text{there exists }i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N},u^{\prime}\in[N]:a_{u^{\prime},s}=i^{\prime}}\text{ for some }s<t

\displaystyle\text{ with }\tau_{I}(i^{\prime})=\tau_{I}(i),\tau_{U}(u^{\prime})=\tau_{U}(u)\Big\}\,.

(7)

Bad recommendations

We now define disjoint events of making bad recommendations corresponding to the four scenarios identified in the prior subsection:

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}&:=\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U}\}\,,\\
\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}&:=\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}})^{c}\}\,,\\
\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}&:=\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}\}\,,\\
\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}&:=\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}\}\,.\end{split}

(8)

These events were described informally near the beginning of Section
4.1
.
The number of bad recommendations up until time
T
is

\displaystyle\mathsf{bad}(T)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\mathds{1}\big\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}\big\}\,.

(9)

4.1.2
Bad Recommendations Induce Regret

The
regularity property
of the preference matrix, defined in Section
4.4.1
, converts uncertainty over columns and rows into uncertainty in the value of the relevant entry. Our i.i.d. preference matrix is easily shown to satisfy this property with probability at least
1-1/(4N)
.

Proposition 4.2

(Bad recommendations are uncertain)
.

Denoting by
\Omega\in\sigma(\Xi)
the event that the preference matrix is regular, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}

\displaystyle=-1|\mathcal{B}^{b}_{u,t},\Omega\big)\geq 1/3\quad\text{for all}\quad b=1,2,3\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}

\displaystyle=-1|\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}\big)\geq 1/2\,.

The proposition for
b=3
will be proved in Section
4.4
and for
b=1,2
and 4 in Appendix
A.3
.
We remark that the proofs rely on the assumption that the entries of the preference matrix are independent. Next corollary is a direct consequence of this proposition and is proved in Appendix
A.4
.

Corollary 4.3
.

The regret is lower bounded as

\mathtt{regret}(T)\geq\frac{1}{3N}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{bad}(T)\right]-\frac{1}{12N}T\,.

4.2
Constraints on Bad Recommendation Scenarios

Constraints on the number of bad recommendations arise for two reasons:

1.

In order for a recommendation to be good (i.e., not bad), any algorithm by definition of the bad scenarios has to have made a certain number of bad recommendations. This constrains the relationship between the number of good and bad recommendations. There are four such constraints, captured by Lemmas
4.4
through
4.7
.

2.

Each user is recommended
T
distinct items. This constrains the total number of recommendations, as captured by Lemma
4.8
.

We now derive the various constraints on the number of bad recommendations.

Constraint I: Weakly Explored Items

We require a bit of notation. As noted when defining
c_{i}^{t}
above, we think of items as being
weakly explored
if they have not been rated by sufficiently many user types to confidently compare them to other items and
strongly explored
if they have. We make the following definitions:

•

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{t}=\{i:c_{i}^{t+1}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I}\}
be the set of items that have been rated by at least
\mathsf{s}_{I}
user types by the end of time
t
and denote its cardinality by

I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{t}:=|\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{t}|=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathds{1}{\{c_{i}^{t+1}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I}\}}\,.

(10)

•

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{t}=\{i:0<c_{i}^{t+1}<\mathsf{s}_{I}\}
be the set items that have been rated by at least one and fewer than
\mathsf{s}_{I}
user types by the end of time
t
. Let
I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{t}
be the number of times any item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{(t)}
has been recommended to a user type for the first time. Note that

I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{t}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}c_{i}^{t+1}\,\,\mathds{1}{\{0<c_{i}^{t+1}<\mathsf{s}_{I}\}}.

(11)

•

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{total}}^{t}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{t}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{t}
be the set of all items that have been recommended to at least one user by the end of time
t
and denote its cardinality by

I_{\mathsf{total}}^{t}:=|I_{\mathsf{total}}^{t}|=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathds{1}{\{0<c_{i}^{t+1}\}}\,.

(12)

We now bound the number of bad recommendations in terms of
I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}
and
I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

The number of bad recommendations
\mathsf{bad}(T)
satisfies

\mathsf{bad}(T)\geq\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\big(\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}\}}\big)\geq I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}\mathsf{s}_{I}+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}\geq I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}\,.

(13)

Proof.

The inequality
I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}\mathsf{s}_{I}+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}\geq I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
is obvious. We proceed with the middle inequality.
First, observe that

\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}\}}\geq\mathds{1}{\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}})^{c}\}}\,.

Recall from (
5
) that
(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}})^{c}
occurs if
u
is the first of its type to rate item
a_{u,t}
.
We will check that

\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\mathds{1}{\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}})^{c}\}}=I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}\mathsf{s}_{I}+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}.

First, consider an item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}
.
For an item to be in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}
, it (by definition) has been rated by at least
\mathsf{s}_{I}
user types, and
the first
\mathsf{s}_{I}
of these each contribute to
\mathds{1}{\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}})^{c}\}}
. Next, consider the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}
.
By definition,
I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}
is precisely the number of contributions to
\mathds{1}{\{c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}})^{c}\}}

∎

Constraint II: Weakly Explored Users

As noted when defining
d_{u}^{t}
above, we think of users as being
weakly explored
if they have not rated sufficiently many item types to compare them to other users.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{t}=\{u\in[N]:d_{u}^{t+1}<\mathsf{s}_{U}\}
be the set of users that have rated fewer than
\mathsf{s}_{U}
distinct item types by the end of time
t
and let

{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{t}:=\frac{1}{N}|\mathcal{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{t}|=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u\in[N]}\mathds{1}{\{d_{u}^{t+1}<\mathsf{s}_{U}\}}

(14)

be the fraction of users in
\mathcal{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{t}
.
Note that since
d_{u}^{t}
is nondecreasing in
t
, for any
u\in\mathcal{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}
we have
d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U}
for all
t\leq T+1
.

Lemma 4.5
.

The number of bad recommendations
\mathsf{bad}(T)
, defined in Equation (
9
), satisfies

\mathsf{bad}(T)\geq\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\big(\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\}}\big)\geq(1-{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T})N\mathsf{s}_{U}\,.

(15)

Proof.

First, observe that

\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\}}\geq\mathds{1}{\{d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}\}}\,.

(16)

Recall from (
6
) that
(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}
is the event that
a_{u,t}
is the first item of its type to be recommended to
u
.
For a user to not be in
\mathcal{W}^{T}
, it (by definition) has rated at least
\mathsf{s}_{U}
item types, and
the first
\mathsf{s}_{U}
of these each contribute to
\mathds{1}{\{d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}\}}
.
∎

Constraint III: New Item Types to (Possibly) Explored Users

It will be useful to consider
\gamma_{*}^{T}
, defined as the minimum over all users
u
, of the number of item types that have been rated by
u
through time
T
. More formally,

\gamma_{*}^{T}:=\min_{u}\Big|\Big\{\textit{j}\in[\texttt{q}_{I}]:\sum_{t\in[T]}\mathds{1}[\tau_{I}(a_{u,t})=j]>0\Big\}\Big|=\min_{u}d_{u}^{T+1}\,.

(17)

The latter equality follows from the definition of
d_{u}^{t}
in (
4
) as the number of item types that have been rated by
u
before time
t
.

Lemma 4.6
.

The number of bad recommendations
\mathsf{bad}(T)
, defined in Equation (
9
), satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{bad}(T)\geq\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\big(\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\}}\big)\geq{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}N\gamma_{*}^{T}\,.

Proof.

For any
u\in\mathcal{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[T]\end{subarray}}\big(\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\}}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[T]\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}{\{d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}\}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}d_{u}^{T+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}\gamma_{*}^{T}\,.

Here (a) follows from (
16
) in the prior lemma; the LHS of (b) is counting the first
\mathsf{s}_{U}
times a new item type is recommended to
u
, and the equality holds since any user
u\in\mathcal{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}
has rated
d_{u}^{T+1}<\mathsf{s}_{U}
item types;
(c) is by (
17
). Summing over the
N{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}
users in
\mathcal{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}
gives the lemma.
∎

Constraint IV: First of an item type to a user type

Let
\pi_{*}
be the maximum number of users of any type:

\pi_{*}=\max_{w\in[\texttt{q}_{U}]}\big|\big\{u\in[N]:\tau_{U}(u)=w\big\}\big|\,.

(18)

The number of
represented user types
, with at least one user of that type, is at least
{N}/{\pi_{*}}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

The number of bad recommendations is bounded as

\mathsf{bad}(T)\geq\gamma_{*}^{T}N/\pi_{*}\,.

Proof.

First, observe that

\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})}\supseteq\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})}\cup\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}}
, from which it follows that

\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\}}+\mathds{1}{\{\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}\}}\geq\mathds{1}{\{(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}\}}\,.

(19)

In words,
(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c}
is the event that
a_{u,t}
is the first item of its type to be recommended to any user of type
\tau_{U}(u)
, which always yields a bad recommendation.
The proof follows directly from definitions (
17
) and (
18
): each of the at least
N/\pi_{*}
represented user types has rated at least
\gamma_{*}^{T}
item types by time
T
.
∎

Constraint V: Total number of recommendations

This step is based on the fact that the total number of recommendations made is
TN
.

Lemma 4.8
.

The number of bad recommendations
\mathsf{bad}(T)
satisfies

\mathsf{bad}(T)\geq TN-\Big[I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}(N-\mathsf{s}_{I})+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}(\pi_{*}-1)\Big]\,.

Proof.

The number of
good
recommendations, i.e., those that are not bad, is
TN-\mathsf{bad}(T)
.

Now, any good recommendation of an item must be either in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}
or
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}
.
Any
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}
can be recommended at most
N
times and as discussed in the proof of Lemma
4.4
, at least
\mathsf{s}_{I}
of these recommendations contribute to
\mathsf{bad}(T)
.
So the total number of good recommendations in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}
is at most
I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}(N-\mathsf{s}_{I})
.
Any
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}
is recommended to
c_{i}^{T+1}
user types, each of which have at most
\pi_{*}
users. The first time
i
is recommended to a user type, it is a bad recommendation
in scenario
\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}
or
\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}
. So the total number of good recommendations of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}
is at most
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}}c_{i}^{T+1}(\pi_{*}-1)=I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}(\pi_{*}-1)
.
∎

4.3
Combining Constraints on Bad Recommendations

Combining Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
gives

\displaystyle\mathsf{bad}(T)

\displaystyle\geq\max\big\{{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}N\gamma_{*}^{T},\,(1-{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T})N\mathsf{s}_{U}\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{0\leq{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}\leq 1}\max\big\{{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T}N\gamma_{*}^{T},\,(1-{U}_{\textsf{weak}}^{T})N\mathsf{s}_{U}\big\}

\displaystyle=N\,\frac{\mathsf{s}_{U}\gamma_{*}^{T}}{\mathsf{s}_{U}+\gamma_{*}^{T}}\geq\frac{1}{2}N\,\min\{\gamma_{*}^{T},\,\mathsf{s}_{U}\}\,.

Combining Lemmas
4.4
and
4.8
yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{bad}(T)

\displaystyle\geq\max\big\{I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}\mathsf{s}_{I}+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T},\,TN-\big[I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}(N-\mathsf{s}_{I})+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}(\pi_{*}-1)\big]\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T},I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}}\max\big\{I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}\mathsf{s}_{I}+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T},\,TN-\big[I_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}(N-\mathsf{s}_{I})+I_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}(\pi_{*}-1)\big]\big\}

\displaystyle\geq T\min\{\mathsf{s}_{I},{N}/{\pi_{*}}\}\,.

The last two displayed equations together with Lemmas
4.4
and
4.7
give

\displaystyle\mathsf{bad}(T)

\displaystyle\geq\max\Big\{I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T},\,\frac{N}{\pi_{*}}\gamma_{*}^{T},\,\frac{N}{2}\min\{\gamma_{*}^{T},\,\mathsf{s}_{U}\},\,T\min\big\{\mathsf{s}_{I},{N}/{\pi_{*}}\big\}\Big\}\,.

(20)

Probabilistic Bounds

The bound in (
20
) is in terms of random variables
\pi_{*}
and
\gamma_{*}^{T}
. We next identify an event of probability
1/4
where these variables can be crudely bounded in terms of the parameters of the model
\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I},N
, and
T
as well as the number
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
of items seen by the algorithm.

Lemma 4.9
.

Assume
N>2\texttt{q}_{U}(\log\texttt{q}_{U})^{2}
and let
\pi_{*}
be as in Eq. (
18
). Then
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\geq 3/4
for the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
defined as

\displaystyle\pi_{*}<{3N}/{\texttt{q}_{U}}\,.

(21)

The proof (in Appendix
B.1
) of this lemma is based on standard balls and bins analysis: each user (ball) has type (bin) independently uniformly distributed on
[\texttt{q}_{U}]
, and
\pi_{*}
is the max load of any bin.

We next relate
\gamma_{*}^{T}
and
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\gamma_{*}^{T}
and
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
be as defined in (
12
) and (
17
). Then
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\geq 1/2
for the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
defined as

\displaystyle I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}\geq\begin{cases}T\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,T/\gamma_{*}^{T}\leq 2\\
\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{I}}/3\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,2<T/\gamma_{*}^{T}\leq 8\log\texttt{q}_{I}\\
T\texttt{q}_{I}/(4\gamma_{*}^{T})\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,8\log\texttt{q}_{I}<T/\gamma_{*}^{T}\,.\end{cases}

(22)

In what follows, we will only use the last two regimes in the above bounds on event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
The proof of this lemma is deferred to Appendix
B.2
, but the intuition is as follows: Each of
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
items has one of
\texttt{q}_{I}
item types, so there are roughly
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}/\texttt{q}_{I}
items of each type. Each user is recommended
T
distinct items of at least
\gamma_{*}^{T}
types, so
\gamma_{*}^{T}I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}/\texttt{q}_{I}\gtrsim T
.
Some care is necessary
due to the adaptive nature of recommendations, and these approximations are made formal by defining appropriate martingales.

Corollary 4.11
.

The expected number of bad recommendations is lower bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathsf{bad}(T)\big]\geq\frac{1}{16}\min_{\gamma\geq 1}\max\big\{f_{1}(\gamma),f_{2}(\gamma),f_{3}(\gamma)\big\}\,.

(23)

where

\displaystyle f_{1}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}T\texttt{q}_{I}/\gamma&\text{ if }\gamma<T/(8\log\texttt{q}_{I})\\
\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{I}}&\text{ if }T/(8\log\texttt{q}_{I})\leq\gamma<T/2\end{cases}\quad\quad f_{2}(\gamma)=\max\big\{\texttt{q}_{U}\gamma,N\min\{\mathsf{s}_{U},\gamma\}\big\}

\displaystyle åf_{3}(\gamma)=T\min\big\{\mathsf{s}_{I},\texttt{q}_{U}\big\}\,.

(24)

Proof.

By the union bound and Lemmas
4.9
and
4.10
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2})\geq 1/4
. On event
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
we can eliminate
\pi_{*}
and
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
from
(
20
). Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathsf{bad}(T)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathsf{bad}(T)\,\big|\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\max\Big\{I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T},\,\frac{N}{\pi_{*}}\gamma_{*}^{T},\,\frac{N}{2}\min\{\gamma_{*}^{T},\,\mathsf{s}_{U}\},\,T\min\big\{\mathsf{s}_{I},{N}/{\pi_{*}}\big\}\Big\}\,\big|\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{4}\max\Big\{f_{1}(\gamma_{*}^{T}),f_{2}(\gamma_{*}^{T}),f_{3}(\gamma_{*}^{T})\Big\}\,\big|\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{16}\min_{\gamma\geq 1}\max\big\{f_{1}(\gamma),f_{2}(\gamma),f_{3}(\gamma)\big\}\,.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qed

Note that the functions
f_{1}(\gamma),f_{2}(\gamma)
and constant function
f_{3}(\gamma)
are parameterized by the parameters of the system
\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I},N
and
T
. So depending on the realization of these parameters, the above minimax takes different forms.
Computing this, although not particularly insightful, and plugging it into Corollary
4.3
immediately gives the lower bound for regret in the following Theorem
4.12
as done in Appendix
B.3
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
\mathsf{s}_{U}
and
\mathsf{s}_{I}
be as defined in Eq. (
25
).
Any recommendation algorithm must incur regret lower bounded as below with numerical constant
c>0

\displaystyle N\mathtt{regret}(T)\geq

\displaystyle c\max\Big\{N,\min\{NT,N\mathsf{s}_{U},\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{I}}\},\min\{\texttt{q}_{U}T,\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{I}}\},

\displaystyle\min\big\{\frac{NT}{\log\texttt{q}_{I}},\sqrt{T\texttt{q}_{I}N},N\mathsf{s}_{U}\big\},\min\{\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}T}{\log\texttt{q}_{I}},\sqrt{T\texttt{q}_{I}\texttt{q}_{U}}\},\,\min\big\{T\mathsf{s}_{I},T\texttt{q}_{U}\big\}\Big\}\,.

This lower bound on regret takes various function forms depending on operating regimes. Figure
6
in Appendix
B.3
shows all possible function forms for this lower bound depending on the model parameters
\texttt{q}_{I},\texttt{q}_{U}
, and
N
.

4.4
Proof of Proposition
4.2

4.4.1
Regularity of Preference Matrix

Suppose the preference matrix
\Xi
is known.
The columns of
\Xi
correspond to different item types. We want to control the uncertainty in a user’s type, i.e. row, given the user’s preferences
\mathbf{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{s}
for some number
s
of item types. Given
\mathbf{x}
, the possible user types are those that have preferences consistent with
\mathbf{x}
.

The following definition captures that the number of these possible types is close to its expectation.
We make use of a bit of notation: For
m\times n
matrix
A
, let
A_{\bullet j}
denote the
j
th column of
A
, and
for ordered tuple of distinct (column) indices
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{s})\in[n]^{s}
, let
A_{\bullet\mathbf{j}}

be the
{m\times s}
matrix formed from the columns of
A
indexed by
\mathbf{j}
. The
i
-th row of this matrix is denoted by
A_{i,\mathbf{j}}
. For given row vector
\mathbf{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{s}
, let

\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(A_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})=\{i\in[m]:A_{i,\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{x}\}

be the indices of rows in
A_{\bullet\mathbf{j}}
that are identical to
\mathbf{x}
.

Definition 4.13

(
(s,\eta)
-column regularity)
.

Fix
s
and
\eta
. Let
A\in\{-1,+1\}^{m\times n}
.
Matrix
A
is said to be
(s,\eta)
-column regular
if

\max_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{j}}\Big|\,|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(A_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|-\frac{m}{2^{s}}\Big|\leq\eta\cdot\frac{m}{2^{s}}\,,

where the maximum is over tuples
\mathbf{j}
of
s
distinct columns and
\mathbf{x}\in\{\pm 1\}^{s}
.
We define
\Omega_{s,\eta}
to be the set of
(s,\eta)
-column regular matrices. We also define
\Omega_{0,\eta}
to be all
\pm 1
matrices.

For capturing uncertainty over the type of an item, given preferences of several user types for it, we define the analogous property for the rows of a matrix.

Definition 4.14

(
(s,\eta)
-row regularity)
.

The matrix
A\in\{-1,+1\}^{n\times m}
is said to be
(s,\eta)
-
row regular
if its transpose
A^{\top}\in\Omega_{s,\eta}
is
(s,\eta)
-column regular.

Claim 1
.

If a matrix
A\in\{-1,+1\}^{m\times n}
is
(s,\eta)
-column regular
, then it is also
(s^{\prime},\eta)
-column regular
for all
s^{\prime}<s
.

This claim follows from the triangle inequality applied to the definition of regularity.
The next lemma states that uniformly random binary matrices are regular with high probability.
Its proof follows easily from Chernoff and union bounds, and is omitted.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let matrix
A\in\{-1,+1\}^{m\times n}
have independent
\mathsf{Unif}(\{-1,+1\})
entries.
If
\eta<1
, then
A
is
(s,\eta)
-column regular with probability at least

\mathbb{P}(A\in\Omega_{s,\eta})\geq 1-2(2n)^{s}\exp\Big(-\frac{\eta^{2}}{3}\frac{m}{2^{s}}\Big)\,.

Corollary 4.16

(Regularity of the preference matrix)
.

Set

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{U}

\displaystyle:=\big\lfloor\log\texttt{q}_{U}-\log\log\texttt{q}_{I}-\log\log N-12\big\rfloor_{+}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{I}

\displaystyle:=\big\lfloor 0.99\log\texttt{q}_{I}-4\log\log N-12\big\rfloor_{+}\,,\qquad\text{and}\quad\eta:={1}/13\,.

(25)

Let
\Omega\in\sigma(\Xi)
be the event that the preference matrix is both
(\mathsf{s}_{U},\eta)
-column regular and
(\mathsf{s}_{I},\eta)
-row regular, i.e.,

\Omega=\{\Xi\in\Omega_{\mathsf{s}_{U},\eta}\}\cap\{\Xi^{T}\in\Omega_{\mathsf{s}_{I},\eta}\}
. Then
\mathbb{P}(\Omega)\geq 1-1/(4N)
.

The proof is immediate using the model assumptions in Figure
2

\texttt{q}_{I},\texttt{q}_{U}>100\log N
,
N>20\texttt{q}_{U}\log^{2}\texttt{q}_{U}
and,
N>100
.

4.4.2
Probability of Liking Item in Terms of Regularity

There are four scenarios to consider in Prop.
4.2
.
Leaving the others to Appendix
A.3
, we focus on the third one (
b=3
) here. The proof of some lemmas used for Prop.
4.2
with
b=3
are also deferred to Appendix
A.2
. This proposition for
b=3
states

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1\,|\,c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c},\Omega)\geq 1/3\,.

(26)

Item
a_{u,t}
, which has been rated by at least
\mathsf{s}_{I}
user types, is being recommended to user
u
who has rated fewer than
\mathsf{s}_{U}
item types, and user
u
has not rated an item of type
\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})
.

It is convenient to interpret the item type function
\tau_{I}:\mathbb{N}\to[\texttt{q}_{I}]
assigning types to the items as a sequence and user type function assigning types to the users
\tau_{U}:[N]\to[\texttt{q}_{U}]
as a vector.
Let
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}:=\{\tau_{U}(u^{\prime}):u^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\{u\}\}
denote the vector of user types for all users except
u
.
Recall that
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
denotes the feedback obtained through time
t-1
.

The next two lemmas express the posterior probability under
(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})})^{c}

of user
u
’s preference for item
i
, conditional on the user feedback
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
obtained so far
as well as the preference matrix
\Xi
, item types
\tau_{I}
, and all user types
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
except for
u^{\prime}s
, in terms of a quantity that is controlled by the column regularity. We emphasize that conditioning on
\Xi,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
yields a simple expression for the posterior in terms of these quantities, but
does
not
mean giving the information to the algorithm producing recommendation
a_{u,t}
.

First, in Lemma
4.17
, we will show that posterior distribution of types of users and items are uniform over the set of types that are
consistent
with the history so far. We use the notation introduced in Section
4.4.1
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Given
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h
in which user
u
has not rated item
i
,
let
\mathbf{j}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the set of item types previously rated by user
u
and row vector
\mathbf{x}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the
\pm 1
feedback.
Similarly, Let
\mathbf{j}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the set of user types that previously rated item
i
and row vector
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the
\pm 1
feedback. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{U}(u)=v,\tau_{I}(i)=j\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)=\frac{\mathds{1}{\{v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}\,\,\frac{\mathds{1}{\{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}))|}\,.

We defer the proof to Appendix
A.2
.
The next Lemma is a simple consequence of the former.
Lemma
4.17
shows that the probability of an event (e.g., item is liked) is given by counting the number of consistent types satisfying the relevant property.
Next, Lemma
4.18
proved in Appendix
A.2.3
expresses these counts in terms of the preference matrix.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\mathbf{j}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I})
and
\mathbf{x}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I})
be as defined in Lemma
4.17
.
Let
\mathbf{x}^{+}
be the vector
\mathbf{x}
appended by
+1
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})})^{c}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet,(\mathbf{j}_{u},\tau_{I}(i))})|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}\,.

This lemma is useful because the count on the RHS can be bounded using regularity of the matrix
\Xi
, as long as
|\mathbf{j}_{u}|
is not too large. We carry this out next.

4.4.3
Proof of Proposition
4.2
with
b=3

We need to show the upper bound in Eq. (
26
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\leq 2/3\,.

(27)

where to shorten the notation, given user
u
and item
i
we defined the event
\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)
and the set
\mathsf{B_{3}}(u,i)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{c_{i}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I},\,d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},\,\big(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})}\big)^{c},\,\Omega\Big\}

(28)

\displaystyle\mathsf{B_{3}}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{\text{realizations of }\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\text{ such that }

\displaystyle\quad\quad\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)\text{ holds}\text{ and }a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t\,\big\}

(29)

be the set of possible realizations of the model parameters and history up to time
t-1
consistent with the event conditioned upon in (
27
).

Set
\mathsf{B_{3}}(u,i)
is well-defined

We note that the condition in (
29
) is a function of the variables
\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)
, so one can determine whether the latter satisfies the former. We spell this out as follows.
The history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
determines whether item
i
has been recommended to user
u
before or not.
The value of
c_{i}^{t}
(as in Definition
3
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all users except user
u
, i.e.,
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}

4
4

4

Conditioning on the event
a_{u,t}=i
implies that
user
u
has not been recommended item
i
by time
t-1
.
Hence ambiguity in the type of user
u
does not make the value of
c_{i}^{t}
ambiguous.
.
Similarly, the value of
d_{u}^{t}
(as in Definition
4
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all items except item
i
, i.e.,
\tau_{I}^{\sim i}
.
Furthermore, the row regularity and column regularity of the preference matrix
\Xi
is a function only of
\Xi
.

Utilizing the regularity property

Let
\mathbf{j}_{u}
and
\mathbf{x}_{u}
be as defined in Lemma
4.17
.
Note that
\mathbf{j}_{u}
is a deterministic function of realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and
\tau_{I}
.
Given any realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and
\tau_{I}\in\mathsf{B_{3}}(u,i)
, the type of user
u
is uniform on
\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})
and
|\mathbf{j}_{u}|=d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U}
.

So for any realization of

\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\in\mathsf{B_{3}}(u,i)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}\big(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{u},\tau_{I}(i))}\big)|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}\big(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}}\big)|}\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{(1+\eta)2^{|\mathbf{j}_{u}|}}{(1-\eta)2^{|\mathbf{j}_{u}|+1}}\overset{(c)}{\leq}2/3\,,

(30)

where (a) uses Lemma
4.18
.
(b) uses the column regularity of matrix
\Xi
(
\Xi\in\Omega_{\mathsf{s}_{U},\eta}
as in Definition
4.13
) and
|\mathbf{j}_{u}|<\mathsf{s}_{U}
. (c) uses

\eta=1/13
.

Applying tower property

Using the total probability lemma on above display, for any
i
such that
a_{u,s}\neq i
for all
s<t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)\big)\leq 2/3\,.

(31)

Recall that there is a random variable
\zeta_{u,t}
, independent of all other variables, such that
a_{u,t}=f_{u,t}(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\zeta_{u,t})
, for some deterministic function
f_{u,t}
.
Also, for all
i
such that
u
has not rated
i
before,
\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\Xi,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
. This is proved using the the same justification as above showing that the set
\mathsf{B_{3}}(u,i)
is well-defined. Clearly,
L_{u,i}\in\sigma(\Xi,\tau_{U},\tau_{I})
.
So conditioning on
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
,
\{a_{u,t}=i\}
is independent of event
\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)
and
L_{u,i}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)\big)\,.

(32)

We use these properties to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,a_{u,t})\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum_{i:a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{3}(u,i)\big)\overset{(c)}{\leq}2/3\,.

Here (a) uses total probability lemma
\{L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\{a_{u,t}=i,L_{u,i}=+1\}
and Eq. (
32
). (b) uses the assumption that an item can be recommended at most once to a user to take the sum only over the items never recommended to
u
before. Using Eq. (
31
) in each term of the sum gives (c).

Using the tower property over
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
on the above display gives the statement of proposition for
b=3
in (
27
).
∎

5
Optimal Recommendation Algorithm and its Approximate Regret: Heuristic Analysis

Theorem
3.1
is somewhat complicated, with a number of different regimes and behaviors.
The aim of this section is to explain how the regret in Theorem
3.1
arises by giving an informal description of our algorithm and a back-of-the-envelope style of heuristic analysis. We defer the formal treatment to Section
6
where we provide detailed pseudocode and Appendix
D
where we prove the associated performance guarantee.

Our recommendation algorithm optimally uses structure in both the item space and user space.
The algorithm makes explicit exploration and exploitation recommendations.
Feedback from the exploration steps allows the algorithm to estimate a portion of the underlying model that is sufficient for making good recommendations in most of the exploitation steps.

There are different types of exploratory recommendations, and each has an associated cost and benefit. Later in this section we will heuristically bound the regret achieved by the algorithm by optimizing the parameters of the algorithm controlling the types of exploration.

5.1
Comparison of Items and Users

As explained in Section
3.1.1

two items
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are declared to be
similar
if a random subset of users
\mathcal{U}
of appropriate size
\mathsf{r}_{I}
rates them identically. Similarly, two users are assumed to be of the same type if they rate
\mathsf{r}_{U}
random items similarly. The choice of
\mathsf{r}_{U}
and
\mathsf{r}_{I}
will be determined later to give small probability of error in clustering items and users.

5.2
Overview of Algorithm

Algorithm
RecommendationSystem
is given below as Algorithm
1
in Section
6.2
. The algorithm has several operating regimes depending on the parameters of the model,
\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I},N
, and time horizon
T
.
Our description will focus on the regime
in which the algorithm utilizes the structure in both item space and user space.
In certain parameter regimes the algorithm makes use of only one of user structure or item structure.

RecommendationSystem
performs the following procedures (not in this order):

User Clustering

A set of items
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
is selected. The items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
are recommended to every user and feedback is received. The
users are then partitioned into clusters, with the users in each cluster having identical feedback for items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
.

Item Clustering

A set
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
of item cluster representatives is selected, forming the cluster centers. A set
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
of items to be clustered is selected. To form the clusters, exploratory recommendations are used to compare items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
to the representatives
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
.

Find Preferences

Each item cluster representative in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
is recommended to one user from each user cluster. The feedback determines the learned preference of each user cluster for each item cluster.

Exploitation

Finally, in the exploitation phase, each ‘like’ feedback in the previous step results in all items in the item cluster being recommended to all users in the user cluster.

5.3
Detailed Algorithm Description and Cost of Exploration

We now explain the main procedures in the algorithm and in parallel determine the cost in regret of each.
As noted above, our description will focus on the regime
in which the algorithm utilizes the structure in both item space and user space.

Recall that regret is the expected number of disliked items recommended per user (
1
).
Recommendations made by our algorithm occur in one of two phases: (i)
Explore
, in which case they turn to be highly uncertain and disliked with probability half, or (ii)
Exploit
, in which case they turn out to be liked with high probability. If there are insufficiently many exploitable items, then the
Exploit
phase ends prematurely and the remaining recommendations are again highly uncertain. Thus, the regret is roughly given by the number of exploratory recommendations, which we call the
cost
of exploration (and which has contributions from several types of exploration), plus the deficit in exploitable items.

Whether or not structure in the item space or user space (or both) is used depends on the
cost versus benefit
of making use of this structure.
The cost of making use of the structure is the number of exploratory recommendations needed to learn. The benefit is in reduced cost for subsequent learning or in obtaining items to be later recommended in the exploit phase.

Algorithm Description

At this point we recommend that the reader glance at the pseudocode in Section
6
.

RecommendationSystem
(Alg.
1
),
as indicated just above, selects three sets
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
, containing
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
and
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
random distinct items.
5
5

5

The use of three sets
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
, as opposed to just one, is to eliminate dependencies and simplify the analysis. In a practical implementation it would make sense to use just one set.
The numbers
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
and
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
are algorithm parameters that will be chosen to minimize regret.

Next, the algorithm calls
Explore
(Alg.
2
) and
Exploit
(Alg.
6
).
Explore
forms the set of exploitable items by using the feedback from calling
UserClustering
(Alg.
3
),
FindPrefs
(Alg.
4
), and
ItemClustering
(Alg.
5
).
We next describe each part of
Explore
and identify the costs associated to each of them.

User Clustering

In
UserClustering
the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
are recommended to every user and feedback is received.
The selected set of items
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
is either of size zero, in which case no recommendations are made in this phase, or of size
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}=\mathsf{r}_{U}
, where

\mathsf{r}_{U}=\lceil 2\log(N\texttt{q}_{U}^{2})\rceil\,.

This is the value of
\mathsf{r}_{U}
discussed in Section
3.1.1
with the choice of error probability bound
\epsilon=1/\texttt{q}_{U}N
.
The
users are then partitioned into clusters, denoted by
\{\mathcal{P}_{w}\}_{w}
, with the users in each cluster
\mathcal{P}_{w}
having identical feedback for items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
. If

{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}=0
, then the clustering is the trivial one with each user in its own cluster and
\mathcal{P}_{w}=\{w\}
.
Otherwise, with
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}=\mathsf{r}_{U}
the choice
\epsilon=1/\texttt{q}_{U}N
gives probability of at least
1-1/N
that the users are clustered correctly according to their types (Lemma
D.1
).

Cost of User Clustering

If user clustering is carried out, then the number of exploratory recommendations is
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
for each user, for a total of

N\cdot{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\approx N\cdot\mathsf{r}_{U}\,.

(33)

Here and below the ‘
\approx
’ denotes approximation up to a (small) constant factor.

Find Preferences

In
FindPrefs
each item cluster representative in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
is recommended to one user from each user cluster. The feedback determines the learned preference of each user cluster for each item cluster. The number of time steps needed is larger for small user clusters than for large ones, and clusters that finish this task are recommended random items until the slowest (smallest) cluster finishes.

Cost of Finding Preferences

The number of users in the smallest cluster is
\min_{w}|\mathcal{P}_{w}|
, hence it takes time
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}/\min_{w}|\mathcal{P}_{w}|
to obtain feedback for all the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
from the smallest user cluster.
Note that if there is no user clustering, then
|\mathcal{P}_{w}|=1
for each
w
, and if the users are clustered according to their types, then
|\mathcal{P}_{w}|\approx N/\texttt{q}_{U}
.
It follows that total number of recommendations made in this exploratory phase is

{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}\cdot\frac{N}{\min_{w}|\mathcal{P}_{w}|}\approx{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}\cdot\big(\texttt{q}_{U}\cdot\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}+N\cdot\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}<\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\big)\,.

(34)
