User clustering enables collaboration to reduce the cost of finding item preferences: to learn the preference of users for an item cluster, one only needs to recommend the representative from the item cluster to one user from each user cluster. This reduces the cost by a factor of
\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{N}
.

Item Clustering

The set of items
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
serve as cluster representatives and the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
are clustered by comparing them to the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
.
The preferences of all user clusters (and hence all users, assuming the user clustering is correct) for the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
are known from the previous step. In
ItemClustering
the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
are recommended to
\mathsf{r}_{I}
random users, where

\mathsf{r}_{I}=\lceil 2\log N\texttt{q}_{I}\rceil\,,

and the feedback is used to cluster the items relative to items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
. The number of comparisons
\mathsf{r}_{I}
ensures that with probability
1-1/N
each item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
is clustered correctly according to its type (Lemma
D.2
). Conceptually, it may be useful to think of recommending the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
to random user clusters, in order to compare the items to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
which already have user cluster preferences. We recommend to random users (rather than random user clusters) to remove dependence and simplify the analysis.

When
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}=0
, the item clustering is the trivial one with each cluster consisting only of its item representative.

Cost of Item Clustering

The number of exploratory recommendations is
\mathsf{r}_{I}
for each item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
, for a total of

{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\cdot\mathsf{r}_{I}\,.

(35)

5.4
Cost of Insufficient Exploration

Here we assume that the clustering of users and items by types is correct, a high probability event due to choice of parameters
\mathsf{r}_{U}
and
\mathsf{r}_{I}
.

Exploiting only a portion of the item space

Recall that the preference of every user is learned for items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
and that the algorithm clusters items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
relative to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
such that items with types not present in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
are discarded in the clustering step.
Thus the size of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
(and the corresponding number of item types in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
) controls both the cost of clustering items and the proportion of items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
that can be clustered and subsequently exploited.

Let
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}=\mathsf{k}({I}_{\mathrm{rep}})
denote the number of distinct item types in the set
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
of item representatives.
This is a random function of
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}=|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}|
, but it turns out that the random fluctuations do not play a significant role in the analysis, so in what follows we treat
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}=\mathsf{k}({I}_{\mathrm{rep}})
as deterministic.
A fraction
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}/\texttt{q}_{I}
of the item space is learned, and in order to balance the cost versus benefit described in the prior paragraph it is sometimes necessary to take
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
strictly less than
\texttt{q}_{I}
.

We next determine how
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
impacts the number of recommendable items from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
.

Obtaining sufficiently many exploitable items

The outcome of all the exploration steps is a set of items to be recommended that the algorithm is confident will be liked. If for a particular user there are not enough items to recommend for the given time horizon
T
, then the algorithm recommends random items to the user, and these items are disliked with probability half.

For each user, the set of exploitable items includes the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
with types that (1) are among the learned types and (2) are liked by the user. A fraction of
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}/\texttt{q}_{I}
of item types are learned and each item type is liked with probability
1/2
. Hence, the number of exploitable items from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
is roughly

{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\cdot{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}/2\texttt{q}_{I}\,.

In addition, the item representatives
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
have been explored by one user per cluster and can be recommended to the other users in the cluster (if any), yielding an additional number of exploitable items per user of

\displaystyle\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\Big(1-\frac{1}{\min_{w}|\mathcal{P}_{w}|}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\Big(1-\frac{1}{N}\Big({\texttt{q}_{U}\cdot\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\atop+\;N\cdot\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}<\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\Big(1-\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{N}\Big)\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\,.

All together, the number of time steps without exploitable items is roughly

\Big(T-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\cdot{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}{2\texttt{q}_{I}}-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\Big(1-\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{N}\Big)\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\Big)_{+}\,.

(36)

Note that for simplicity we have ignored the time steps consumed by exploration, as this does not significantly change the outcome.
Due to the assumption
2\texttt{q}_{U}\log\texttt{q}_{U}<N
, the factor of
(1-\texttt{q}_{U}/N)
in the last term of (
36
) is a constant and is removed in (
37
).

5.5
Regret in Terms of Costs

Fix algorithm parameters
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
. Recall that
\mathsf{r}_{U}
and
\mathsf{r}_{I}
are functions of the number of types
\texttt{q}_{U}
and
\texttt{q}_{I}
and represent the number of comparisons needed to determine similarity of a pair of users or items, respectively.
The total cost of exploration plus the cost due to insufficiently many exploitable items as determined in
Equations (
33
), (
34
), (
35
), and (
36
)
leads to a regret of

\displaystyle 2N

\displaystyle\cdot\mathtt{regret}(T)\;\approx\;

(37)

\displaystyle N\cdot{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}+

\displaystyle{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}\Big({\texttt{q}_{U}\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\atop+N\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}<\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}}\Big)+{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\mathsf{r}_{I}+N\Big(T-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}{2\texttt{q}_{I}}-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\Big)_{+}\,.

Here
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}=\mathsf{k}({I}_{\mathrm{rep}})
, hence the total regret is a function of the algorithm parameters
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
. The factor of 2 in front of the regret on the left-hand side is due to random recommendations being liked with probability
1/2
.

Understanding Cost Versus Benefit

Before optimizing (
37
) over
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
, we informally examine the cost versus benefit associated to varying each of these parameters.

To begin, we see that increasing
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
to
\mathsf{r}_{U}

allows to cluster the users and reduces the second term, the cost of learning the user preferences for the item representatives, because only a single user in each cluster needs to give feedback for each item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
. The fourth term also decreases, since items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
are recommended to other users from clusters where the selected user liked the item.
The cost of increasing
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
is reflected in the first term, since all users are recommended all items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
.

Next, increasing
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
grows the set of items that are clustered to the representatives
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
. This yields, for each of the
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
item types represented in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
, on the order of
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}/\texttt{q}_{I}
items that can be recommended in the exploit phase. This is reflected in the fourth term. The third term is the cost of clustering these items,
\mathsf{r}_{I}
for each item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
.

Finally, increasing

{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
improves the fourth term in two ways: first by increasing the number of items
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
to be exploited in the case that the users are clustered, and second by increasing the number
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
of item types in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
which increases the proportion of items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
which can be usefully clustered to item representatives and which in turn results in more exploitable items.
The cost of increasing
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
appears in the second term in (
37
), and is based on each user cluster rating each item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
.

The effects of changing the algorithm parameters are evidently inter-related, and we optimize them in the next subsection.

5.6
Optimizing the Costs

The algorithm parameters
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
are chosen to minimize the total regret in (
37
). The optimal choice depends on the system parameters
N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I}
and there are different algorithm operating regimes depending on these parameters. We return to the regimes momentarily, but first make a simplifying observation.

The factor
N
in front of the last term in (
37
) is so large that
it is optimal to choose
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
and
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
to make the last term zero,
i.e., to ensure that the set of exploitable items is large enough that no random recommendations are made in the
Exploit
phase.
Intuitively, this is because if random recommendations are going to be made at the end of the
Exploit
phase, one might as well instead increase the
Explore
phase with no penalty.
Hence, to minimize regret, the parameters
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
are chosen to minimize the total cost of exploration subject to the constraint

T\leq\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}{2\texttt{q}_{I}}+\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}

and taking into consideration
the third term of (
37
) leads to the optimal choice

{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}=\frac{2\texttt{q}_{I}}{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}\Big(T-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\Big)_{+}\,.

(38)

Plugging into (
37
) we get

\displaystyle 2N\cdot\mathtt{regret}(T)\;\approx\;

\displaystyle\qquad N\cdot{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}+{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}\cdot\Big({\texttt{q}_{U}\cdot\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\atop+\;N\cdot\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}<\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}}\Big)+\frac{2\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}\Big(T-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\mathds{1}{\{{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}}\Big)_{+}\,.

(39)

It remains to optimize over
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
and
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
.
Starting with
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
, note that it is always optimal to set
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
to one of
\mathsf{r}_{U}
or 0. The former corresponds to using the user structure while the latter corresponds to ignoring the user structure. Explicitly,

{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}=\mathsf{r}_{U}\cdot\mathds{1}{\Big\{N\mathsf{r}_{U}+{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}\texttt{q}_{U}+\frac{2\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}\Big(T-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\Big)_{+}<{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}N+\frac{2\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}T\Big\}}\,.

(40)

Plugging into (
39
) yields

\displaystyle 2N\cdot\mathtt{regret}(T)\;

\displaystyle\approx\min\Big\{N\mathsf{r}_{U}+{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}\texttt{q}_{U}+\frac{2\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}\Big(T-\frac{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}{2}\Big)_{+},\,{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}N+\frac{2\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}T\Big\}\,.

(41)

For the purpose of the slightly heuristic exposition of this section we make the approximation (accurate within a factor of
\log\texttt{q}_{I}
)

{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}=\mathsf{k}({I}_{\mathrm{rep}})\approx\min\{{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}/2,\texttt{q}_{I}\}\,.

(42)

(See Lemma
E.4
for a more accurate bound.)
Recall that
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
is an algorithm design parameter that can be optimized over.
Plugging (
42
) into (
41
) and
minimizing over
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
(after some simplification, see Appendix
C
) yields

\mathtt{regret}(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\approx\min\{f(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I}),g(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\}\,,

(43)

where

\displaystyle f(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{r}_{U}/2+\texttt{q}_{U}N^{-1}T,\quad&\text{if }T\leq\texttt{q}_{I}\frac{\mathsf{r}_{I}}{\texttt{q}_{U}}\text{ or }\texttt{q}_{U}\leq\mathsf{r}_{I}\\
\vskip 2.0pt\cr\mathsf{r}_{U}/2+2N^{-1}\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{U}\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}T}-\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}N^{-1},\quad&\text{if }\texttt{q}_{I}\frac{\mathsf{r}_{I}}{\texttt{q}_{U}}<T\leq\texttt{q}_{I}\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{\mathsf{r}_{I}}\\
\vskip 2.0pt\cr\mathsf{r}_{U}/2+\texttt{q}_{I}N^{-1}(\texttt{q}_{U}-\mathsf{r}_{I})+\mathsf{r}_{I}N^{-1}T,\quad&\text{if }\texttt{q}_{I}\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{\mathsf{r}_{I}}<T\text{ and }\texttt{q}_{U}>\mathsf{r}_{I}\end{cases}

\displaystyle g(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})=\begin{cases}2\sqrt{N^{-1}\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}T},\quad&\text{if }T\leq\texttt{q}_{I}\frac{N}{\mathsf{r}_{I}}\\
\vskip 2.0pt\cr\texttt{q}_{I}+\mathsf{r}_{I}N^{-1}T,\quad&\text{if }T>\texttt{q}_{I}\frac{N}{\mathsf{r}_{I}}\,.\end{cases}

In the next subsection we refer to the five functions appearing in
f
and
g
as

\mathsf{reg}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{reg}_{5}
(in the order they appear).

5.7
Algorithm Operating Regimes

The regret as given in
(
43
)
is a piece-wise smooth function of the system parameters
N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I}
, with each of the five pieces corresponding to a region of the parameter space:

S_{i}=\{(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:\mathtt{regret}(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})=\mathsf{reg}_{i}(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\}\,.

The optimizer
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}
of (
41
) as shown in Appendix
C
is also a piecewise smooth function of the system parameters
N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I}
(corresponding to the same partition of the parameter space) and hence so too are the optimal values
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}
and
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{*}
obtained from the preceding Equations (
38
) and (
40
).

Recall that the model for user preferences consists of latent
types for the users, latent types for the items, and an unknown preference matrix specifying for each user type and item type whether a user of that type likes an item of that type.
The algorithm learns various parts of the model (and to varying degrees) depending on the values of the algorithm parameters
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*},{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{*},
and
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}
.
We now explain how the algorithm behavior corresponds to each choice of algorithm parameter (deferring the derivations leading to these choices to Appendix
C
and Equations (
38
) and (
40
)).

User-user regime

When
(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\in S_{1}
, i.e., regret in (
43
) is given by
\mathsf{reg}_{1}
,

{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}=2T,\quad{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}=\mathsf{r}_{U},\quad\text{and }\quad{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{*}=0\,.

The users are clustered based on the
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}=\mathsf{r}_{U}
recommendations made to each for that purpose, i.e., the algorithm learns the structure in the user space.
After clustering the users, the algorithm learns the preference of each user cluster for the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
by recommending each to one user per cluster.
If a user likes an item, then the item is recommended to the rest of the user’s cluster in the exploit phase. Note that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
is empty in this regime, meaning that the clustering of items is trivial with the representatives
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
each in its own cluster.

Hybrid partially-learned regime

When
(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\in S_{2}
, i.e.,
regret in (
43
) is given by
\mathsf{reg}_{2}
, it turns out that

{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}=2\sqrt{{T\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}/{\texttt{q}_{U}}},\quad{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}=\mathsf{r}_{U},\quad\text{and }\quad{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{*}=2\sqrt{T\texttt{q}_{I}\texttt{q}_{U}/\mathsf{r}_{I}}-2\texttt{q}_{I}\,.

After clustering the users, the algorithm learns the preference of each user cluster for the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
by recommending each to one user per cluster.
The algorithm then learns structure in the item space,
clustering items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
to the representatives
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
.

Only a portion of the item space is learned
,
because
\mathsf{k}({I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*})
, the number of learned item types, is smaller than
\texttt{q}_{I}
:
when regret in (
43
) is given by
\mathsf{reg}_{2}
,
T<\texttt{q}_{I}\texttt{q}_{U}/\mathsf{r}_{I}
, which amounts to
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}=2\sqrt{{T\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}/{\texttt{q}_{U}}}<2\texttt{q}_{I}
. Plugging into (
42
) gives
\mathsf{k}({I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*})<\texttt{q}_{I}
.

Hybrid fully-learned regime

When
(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\in S_{3}
, i.e., regret in (
43
) is given by
\mathsf{reg}_{3}
,

{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}=2\texttt{q}_{I},\quad{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}=\mathsf{r}_{U},\quad\text{and }\quad{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{*}=2T-2\texttt{q}_{I}\,.

The algorithm again clusters the users but now learns the preferences for
all
item types. The fact that all of the item space is learned is due to
\mathsf{k}({I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*})=\texttt{q}_{I}
, i.e., with high probability every item type is represented in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
.

Item-item partially-learned regime

When
(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\in S_{4}
, i.e.,
regret in (
43
) is given by
\mathsf{reg}_{4}
,

{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}=2\sqrt{{\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}T}/{N}},\quad{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}=0,\quad\text{and }\quad{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{*}=2\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{I}TN/\mathsf{r}_{I}}\,.

Because
{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}=0
, no user clustering occurs and structure in the user space is ignored.
A portion of the item space is learned as in the regime
S_{1}
above, except that now this learning occurs without the benefit of collaboration between the users.

Item-item fully-learned regime

When
(N,T,\texttt{q}_{U},\texttt{q}_{I})\in S_{5}
, i.e.,
regret in (
43
) is given by
\mathsf{reg}_{5}
,

{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}^{*}=2\texttt{q}_{I},\quad{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}^{*}=0,\quad\text{and }\quad{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{*}=2T\,.

The users are not clustered and all of the item space is learned by recommending the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
to all users.

6
Algorithm Pseudocode

6.1
Notation Used in the Algorithm

For an item
i
and time
t>0
, define

\mathsf{rated}_{t}(i)=\{u\in[N]:a_{u,s}=i\text{ for some }s<t\}

is the set of users that have rated item
i
before time
t
. We use the notation
\mathsf{rated}(i)
in the algorithm for
\mathsf{rated}_{t}(i)
at the time it is used.
The partitioning (clustering) over the users is denoted by
\mathcal{P}
.
The notation
L_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
denotes the preference of user clusters for items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
.
The sets
\mathcal{S}_{\textit{j}}
are the clusters of items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}

which are declared to be of the same type as
\textit{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
, and also include item
j
.
Note that several clusters of items
\mathcal{S}_{\textit{j}}
’s, can overlap, i.e., a given item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
can belong to multiple clusters if there are two or more representative items of the same type.
For each user
u
there is a set of exploitable items
\mathcal{R}_{u}\,
formed in Lines
2
of
Explore
, which consists of the items in the groups
\mathcal{S}_{\textit{j}}
whose representative
j
were liked by
u
or a user from the same user cluster as
u
.

6.2
Algorithm Pseudocode

We now give the pseudocode for
RecommendationSystem
and its subroutines.

Input:

(N,T,\texttt{q}_{I},\texttt{q}_{U})

Set global algorithm parameters
\mathsf{r}_{U},\mathsf{r}_{I},{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
, and
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
as in Section
6.3
.

1

//
Form sets of items to be used for exploration

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\leftarrow{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
new random items
//
items used to cluster users

2

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}\leftarrow{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
new random items
//
item cluster representatives

3

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\leftarrow{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
new random items
//
items to be clustered

4

//
Explore in order to form sets of exploitable items

\{\mathcal{R}_{u}\}_{u\in[N]}\leftarrow\textsc{Explore}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}})

5

//
Recommend exploitable items

\textsc{Exploit}(\{\mathcal{R}_{u}\}_{u\in[N]})

Algorithm 1

RecommendationSystem

1

Input:

(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}})

Output:

A set
\{\mathcal{R}_{u}\}_{u\in[N]}
to be recommended to each user

2

//
Cluster the users

\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\textsc{UserClustering}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}})

3

//
Learn preference of user clusters
\mathcal{P}_{w}
for cluster centers in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}

L_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}\leftarrow\textsc{FindPrefs}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}},\mathcal{P})

4

//
Cluster the items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
to cluster centers
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}

\{\mathcal{S}_{\textit{j}}\}_{\textit{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}\leftarrow\textsc{ItemClustering}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},\mathcal{P},L_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}})

5

//
Form sets of good items for each user

for

w=1,\dots,|\mathcal{P}|

do

6

\mathcal{R}_{u}\leftarrow\bigcup_{j\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}:L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},\textit{j}}=+1}\mathcal{S}_{j}
for each
u

such that
u\in\mathcal{P}_{w}

return

\{\mathcal{R}_{u}\}_{u\in[N]}

Algorithm 2

Explore

1

Input:

Items
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
to be used for user clustering

Output:

A partition
\mathcal{P}
of the users

2

Recommend all items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
to all users

3

if

{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}\geq\mathsf{r}_{U}

then

4

5

Let
\{\mathcal{P}_{w}\}_{w}
be the partition of the users
into fewest possible groups (clusters) such that users in each group agree on all items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}

else

6

\mathcal{P}_{w}\leftarrow\{w\}
for
w\in[N]

//
trivial user clustering

7

return

\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{w}\}_{w}

Algorithm 3

\textsc{UserClustering}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{usr}})

1

Input:

Item representatives
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
and user clusters
\mathcal{P}

Output:

Preferences
L_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
of user clusters for item representatives

2

//
Determine rating
L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},\textit{j}}
of each user cluster
\mathcal{P}_{w}
for each item cluster representative
\textit{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}

do in parallel for

w=1,\dots,|\mathcal{P}|

3

for

\textit{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}

do

4

a_{u,t}\leftarrow\textit{j}

for any available user
u
in
\mathcal{P}_{w}
and
let
L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},\textit{j}}=L_{u,\textit{j}}

//
Rmk.
6.1

Until all user clusters are finished exploring
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
, recommend random items to users in
\mathcal{P}_{w}

return

L_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}}

Algorithm 4

\textsc{FindPrefs}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}},\mathcal{P})

Input:

(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},\mathcal{P},L_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}})

Output:

\{\mathcal{S}_{\textit{j}}\}_{\textit{j}}

//
Preliminary exploration of exploitable items in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}

Recommend each item
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
to
\mathsf{r}_{I}
random users

//
see Rmk.
6.2

//
Record prefs
(L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},i})
of user clusters for items
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}

1

For each obtained
L_{u,i}
, record pref of
u
’s cluster
\mathcal{P}_{w}
as
L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},i}=L_{u,i}

//
Rmk.
6.3

2

//
Cluster
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
around centers
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}

for

\textit{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}

do

3

4

\mathcal{S}_{\textit{j}}\leftarrow\{\textit{j}\}\cup\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}:L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},i}=L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},\textit{j}}
for all
w
such that
\mathsf{rated}(i)\cap\mathcal{P}_{w}\neq\varnothing
}

return

\{\mathcal{S}_{\textit{j}}\}_{\textit{j}}

Algorithm 5

\textsc{ItemClustering}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},\mathcal{P},L_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}})

1

Input:

\{\mathcal{R}_{u}\}_{u\in[N]}

2

for

remaining
t\leq T

do

3

for

u\in[N]

do

4

if

there is an item
i\in\mathcal{R}_{u}
such that
u\notin\mathsf{rated}(i)
,

then

5

6

a_{u,t}\leftarrow i

else

7

a_{u,t}\leftarrow
a random item not yet rated by
u

Algorithm 6

Exploit

Remark 6.1

(Find preference of user clusters for item representatives)
.

At each time
t
, for each user
u
, assign an item
j\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
such that no other user in the same partition as
u
has been recommended
j
before time
t
, or already assigned to
j
at time
t
. If no such item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
exists, assign a random new item to
u
.

Remark 6.2

(Assignment of users to items in Line
5
of
ItemClustering
)
.

Recommending each item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
to
\mathsf{r}_{I}
random users is done over
\lceil{{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\mathsf{r}_{I}}/{N}\rceil
time-steps, with additional
N\lceil{{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\mathsf{r}_{I}}/{N}\rceil-{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\mathsf{r}_{I}
recommendations being random new items.
Concretely, randomly permute the users, form a list by repeating the same permutation
\lceil{{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}\mathsf{r}_{I}}/{N}\rceil
times, and then assign each item in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}
to successive contiguous blocks of
\mathsf{r}_{I}
users from the list.

Remark 6.3

(User cluster preferences in Line
5
of
ItemClustering
)
.

In case there is an error in the user clustering and there are contradictory preferences from two different users from the same cluster for a given item, then the recorded cluster preference
L_{\mathcal{P}_{w},i}
can be arbitrary. Note that the preferences of some user clusters for some items remains unknown.

6.3
Choice of Algorithm Parameters

We now specify the values of parameters used in the algorithm pseudocode. Let

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}_{U}

\displaystyle=\lceil 2\log(N\texttt{q}_{U}^{2})\rceil,\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathsf{r}_{I}=\big\lceil 2\log(N\texttt{q}_{I})\big\rceil\,.

(44)

It is convenient to separate the algorithm operation into two broad operating regimes depending on whether or not structure in the item space is used.

6.3.1
System parameters
I^{\mathsf{NoItemClust}}

Let functions
\mathtt{R}_{U}(T)
and
\mathtt{R}_{I}(T)
be defined in (
49
) and (
50
).
In the regime of parameters in which
\mathtt{R}_{U}(T)\leq\mathtt{R}_{I}(T)
(in which item clustering does not occur)
the algorithm
RecommendationSystem
uses the following values for
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
and
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
referred to collectively as
I^{\mathsf{NoItemClust}}
:

\displaystyle{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}=0,\quad{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}=\mathsf{r}_{U},\quad{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}=6T\,.

(45)

6.3.2
System parameters
I^{\mathsf{ItemClust}}

Let functions
\mathtt{R}_{U}(T)
and
\mathtt{R}_{I}(T)
be defined in (
49
) and (
50
).
In the regime of parameters in which
\mathtt{R}_{U}(T)>\mathtt{R}_{I}(T)
(in which item clustering occurs)
the algorithm
RecommendationSystem
uses the following values for
{I}_{\mathrm{exp}},{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}
and
{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}
referred to collectively as
I^{\mathsf{ItemClust}}
:

\displaystyle{I}_{\mathrm{exp}}

\displaystyle=\left\lceil 16\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}}{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}}T\right\rceil,\,{I}_{\mathrm{usr}}=\mathsf{r}_{U}\mathds{1}{\{{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}>\mathsf{r}_{U}\}},\,{I}_{\mathrm{rep}}=\begin{cases}\lceil 3{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}\rceil,&\text{if }{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}\leq\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}}{3}\\
\lceil\texttt{q}_{I}\log(N\texttt{q}_{I})\rceil,&\text{if }{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}>\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}}{3}\end{cases}\,.

(46)

where the parameter
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Item}}\quad&\text{ if }\,\,\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Item}}\leq\mathsf{r}_{U}\,,\\
\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Hybrid}},&\text{ if }\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Item}}>\mathsf{r}_{U}\quad\text{and }\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Hybrid}}\leq\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}}{3}\\
\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Asymp}},&\text{ if }\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Item}}>\mathsf{r}_{U}\quad\text{and }\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Hybrid}}>\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}}{3}\,,\end{cases}

(47)

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Item}}

\displaystyle=16\log T+2\sqrt{\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}{N}T},\quad\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Hybrid}}=8\mathsf{r}_{U}+2\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{r}_{I}\texttt{q}_{I}T}{\texttt{q}_{U}}}\,,\text{ and}\quad\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Asymp}}=\texttt{q}_{I}\,.

(48)

The above discontiuities in the definition of parameter
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
as a function of time horizon
T
can be interpreted as follows:
The parameter
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
is a nondecreasing function of
T
where for three different regimes
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}\leq\mathsf{r}_{U}
,
\mathsf{r}_{U}<{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}\leq\texttt{q}_{I}/3
and
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}>\texttt{q}_{I}/3
the parameter
{\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{rep}}}
has different dependencies on
T
.

6.4
Upper Bound on Regret for
RecommendationSystem

The following theorem gives the upper bound on the regret of the proposed algorithm
RecommendationSystem
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Define the parameters
\mathsf{r}_{U}
and
\mathsf{r}_{I}
as in Equation (
44
). Then the following bounds for regret of the
RecommendationSystem
algorithm holds:

\mathtt{regret}(T)\leq\min\Big\{\frac{T}{2},\,\,C\mathtt{R}_{U}(T),C\mathtt{R}_{I}(T)\Big\}

for a constant
C
where the functions
\mathtt{R}_{U}(T)
and
\mathtt{R}_{I}(T)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathtt{R}_{U}(T)=\mathsf{r}_{U}+\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{N}T

(49)

and

\displaystyle\mathtt{R}_{I}(T)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\vskip 7.22743pt\log T+\sqrt{\frac{\texttt{q}_{I}\mathsf{r}_{I}}{N}T},&\text{ if }T<\mathsf{T}_{Item}\\
\vskip 7.22743pt\mathsf{r}_{U}+\frac{1}{N}\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{I}\texttt{q}_{U}\mathsf{r}_{I}T},&\text{ if }\mathsf{T}_{Item}\leq T<\mathsf{T}_{Hybrid}\\
\mathsf{r}_{U}+\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{N}\texttt{q}_{I}\log(N\texttt{q}_{I})+\frac{\mathsf{r}_{I}}{N}T,&\text{ if }\mathsf{T}_{Hybrid}\leq T\,.\end{cases}

(50)

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{Item}

\displaystyle:=\max\{T:\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Item}}\leq\mathsf{r}_{U}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{T}_{Hybrid}:=\max\{T:\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Hybrid}}\leq\texttt{q}_{I}/3\}\,

for
\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Item}}
and
\mathsf{k}_{\mathrm{Hybrid}}
defined in (
48
).

Appendix A
Deferred Proofs from Section
4.1

In this section, we prove the lemmas in Section
4.1
used to identify the bad recommendations and their associated scenarios in Proposition
4.2
for
b=1,2,3
and 4 which characterizes the bad recommendations. Next, we tie these scenarios to the definition of regret (Cor.
4.3
) proved in Section
A.4
.

A.1
Notation

In this section it is convenient to interpret the item type function
\tau_{I}:\mathbb{N}\to[\texttt{q}_{I}]
assigning types to the items as a sequence and user type function assigning types to the users
\tau_{U}:[N]\to[\texttt{q}_{U}]
as a vector.
Let
\tau_{I}^{\sim i}:=\{\tau_{I}(i^{\prime}):i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N},i^{\prime}\neq i\}
denote the sequence of item types for all items except
i
, with
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
defined analogously.

We use

\Xi^{\sim(w,\textit{j})}=\{\xi_{w^{\prime},\textit{j}^{\prime}}:(w^{\prime},\textit{j}^{\prime})\in[\texttt{q}_{U}]\times[\texttt{q}_{I}],(w^{\prime},\textit{j}^{\prime})\neq(w,\textit{j})\}
to denote the set of all elements in matrix
\Xi
except
\xi_{w,\textit{j}}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
denotes the feedback obtained through time
t-1
.

When user
u
has rated an item with type
j
, for the sake of brevity, we say ‘user
u
has rated item type
j
’.
Similarly, when item
i
has been rated by a user with user type
w
, we say ‘item
i
has been rated by user type
w
’.

A.2
Missing Lemma Proofs from Section
4.4

The proof of Lemma
4.17
rests on a simple probabilistic statement about the posterior over histories. We start with the latter.

A.2.1
Probabilities of System Trajectories

Lemma A.1
.

Given
\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t}
in which user
u
has not rated item
i
,
let
\mathbf{j}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the set of item types previously rated by user
u
and row vector
\mathbf{x}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the
\pm 1
feedback.
Similarly, Let
\mathbf{j}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the set of user types that previously rated by item
i
and row vector
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the
\pm 1
feedback.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t},\,\big|\,

\displaystyle\tau_{U}(u)=v,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle=c\cdot\mathds{1}{\{v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}\big(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}}\big)(h_{t},\Xi,\tau_{I}^{\sim i}),j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\big(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}\big)(h_{t},\Xi,\tau_{U}^{\sim u})\}}

for some
c
that does not depend on
v
or
j
.

Proof.

The history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
includes all the items recommended and the associated
\pm 1
feedback up to and including time
t
; its distribution is complicated, but we do not need to calculate the posterior probability of
\mathcal{H}_{t}
in the lemma statement.

Fix
\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
, as well as a specific possible trajectory for the history
h_{t}
in which user
u
has not rated item
i
.
We consider two copies of the history random process
\mathcal{H}_{t}
: the first
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{1}
, where
\tau_{U}(u)=v_{1},\tau_{I}(i)=j_{1}
, and the second
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{2}
where
\tau_{U}(u)=v_{2},,\tau_{I}(i)=j_{2}
, where both
v_{1},v_{2}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}\big(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}}\big)(h_{t},\Xi,\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
and both
j_{1},j_{2}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\big(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}\big)(h_{t},\Xi,\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
. I.e., both user types and item types are consistent with the revealed preferences of user
u
and item
i
in the history
h_{t}
. For all users
u^{\prime}
(including
u
) and times
s\leq t
, the recommended item is described by a function
a_{u^{\prime},s}=f_{u^{\prime},s}(\mathcal{H}_{s-1},\zeta_{u^{\prime},s})
for some auxiliary independent random variable
\zeta_{u^{\prime},s}
. We thus have two copies of all these variables,
a_{u^{\prime},s}^{1}
and
a_{u^{\prime},s}^{2}
, and so forth. This shows that all consistent histories have the same probabilities, proving the lemma.

We will give an inductive argument. Suppose that one of these two cases holds for some
s,0\leq s\leq t-1
:

1.

\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}=\mathcal{H}_{s}^{2}=h_{s}
, or

2.

Both
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}\neq h_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{2}\neq h_{s}
.

We will show below that at time
s+1
one of these two cases continues to hold.
Note that the first case is tautologically true for
s=0
, since then the histories are empty, which serves as our base case. By induction, one of the two cases holds at time
t
, which implies that for any
h_{t}
and any
v_{1},v_{2}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}\big(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}}\big)(h_{t},\Xi,\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
and
j_{1},j_{2}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\big(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}\big)(h_{t},\Xi,\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t}\,\big|\,

\displaystyle\tau_{U}(u)=v_{1},\tau_{I}(i)=j_{1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t}\,\big|\,\tau_{U}(u)=v_{2},\tau_{I}(i)=j_{2},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big).

We now prove the inductive step.
First, observe that if case 2 holds above, i.e., the histories both differ from
h_{s}
, then case 2 continues to hold for all future times.

Now suppose that case 1 holds at time
s
.
We couple the
\zeta_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{1}
and
\zeta_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{2}
random variables to be equal for all
u^{\prime}
, which results in
a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{1}=a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{2}
, i.e., all recommendations made at step
s+1
in both processes are the same. These both agree with
h_{s+1}
, or both disagree with
h_{s+1}
, and the latter case puts us into case 2 at time
s+1
.

In the former case where
a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{1}=a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{2}
agree with
h_{s+1}
, the feedback
L_{u^{\prime},a_{u^{\prime},s+1}}^{1}
and
L_{u^{\prime},a_{u^{\prime},s+1}}^{2}
from these recommendations is the same in both
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{2}
copies:

1.

For users
u^{\prime}\neq u
such that
a_{u^{\prime},s+1}\neq i
the types are the same in both copies and hence preferences are the same, as both
\Xi
and the types of these users and the recommended items are fixed.

2.

For users
u^{\prime}\neq u
such that
a_{u^{\prime},s+1}=i
, the user types are the same in both copies and item
i
’s type
j_{1},j_{2}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\big(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}\big)
by definition of
\Lambda
guarantees that the actual preferences of these users are the same for
i
in
h_{s+1}
.

3.

For user
u
, its type is different in the two copies, however, user
u
’s type
v_{1},v_{2}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}\big(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}}\big)
by definition of
\Lambda
guarantees that the actual preferences are the same for items
a_{u,s+1}
recommended in
h_{s+1}
.

This completes the induction argument.
∎

A.2.2
Proof of Lemma
4.17
: Uniform Posterior Over Types

Lemma
4.17
.

Given
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h
in which user
u
has not rated item
i
,
let
\mathbf{j}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the set of item types previously rated by user
u
and row vector
\mathbf{x}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the
\pm 1
feedback.
Similarly, Let
\mathbf{j}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the set of user types that previously rated item
i
and row vector
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the
\pm 1
feedback. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{U}(u)=v,\tau_{I}(i)=j\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)=\frac{\mathds{1}{\{v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}\,\,\frac{\mathds{1}{\{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}))|}\,.

Proof.

Bayes’ rule, the fact that
\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i)
and
\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
are independent (and
\tau_{U}(u)\sim\mathsf{unif}([\texttt{q}_{U}])
,
\tau_{I}(i)\sim\mathsf{unif}([\texttt{q}_{I}])
), and then Lemma
A.1
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{U}(u)=v,\tau_{I}(i)=j\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t}=h,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h\big|\,\tau_{U}(u)=v,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\,\,\mathbb{P}\big(\tau_{U}(u)=v,\tau_{I}(i)=j\big)}{\sum_{v^{\prime}\in[\texttt{q}_{U}],j^{\prime}\in[\texttt{q}_{I}]}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h\big|\,\tau_{U}(u)=v^{\prime},\tau_{I}(i)=j^{\prime},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\mathbb{P}\big(\tau_{U}(u)=v^{\prime},\tau_{I}(i)=j^{\prime}\big)}

\displaystyle=\frac{c\cdot\mathds{1}{\{v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}}),j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})\}}}{\sum_{v^{\prime}\in[\texttt{q}_{U}],j^{\prime}\in[\texttt{q}_{I}]}c\cdot\mathds{1}{\{v^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}}),j^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})\}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{1}{\{v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}\,\,\frac{\mathds{1}{\{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})|}\,,

where in the last line we used
\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})\in\sigma(h,\Xi,\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
and
\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\in\sigma(h,\Xi,\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
and hence are independent of
j
and
v
.

∎

A.2.3
Proof of Lemma
4.18
: Probability of Liked Item In Terms of Regularity

Lemma
4.18
.

Let
\mathbf{j}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I})
and
\mathbf{x}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I})
be as defined in Lemma
4.17
.
Let
\mathbf{x}^{+}
be the vector
\mathbf{x}
appended by
+1
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})})^{c}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet,(\mathbf{j}_{u},\tau_{I}(i))})|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}\,.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{j}=\mathbf{j}_{u}
and
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{u}
.
Using
\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I})
and Lemma
4.17

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{U}(u)=v\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h,(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})})^{c}\,,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\tau_{U}(u)=v\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h,,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{1}{\{v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|}\,.

Given the items upon which we are conditioning in the lemma statement the only uncertainty is in the type of user
u
, and moreover

L_{u,i}=+1
if and only if
\tau_{U}(u)\in\Lambda_{+}(\Xi_{\bullet\tau_{I}(i)})=\{v\in[\texttt{q}_{U}]:\xi_{v,\tau_{I}(i)}=+1\}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})})^{c}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\Lambda_{+}(\Xi_{\bullet\tau_{I}(i)})\cap\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|}\,.

Using Definition
6
, on the event
\big(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{{u},\tau_{I}({i})}\big)^{c}
,

\tau_{I}(i)
is not among the item types rated by
u
, i.e.,

\tau_{I}(i)\notin\mathbf{j}
. It follows that,
|\Lambda_{+}(\Xi_{\bullet\tau_{I}(i)})\cap\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|=|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}^{+}}\big(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j},\tau_{I}(i))}\big)|
.
∎

A.3
Proof of Prop
4.2
for
b=1,2,4

A.3.1
Proof of Proposition
4.2
with
b=1

Proposition
4.2
with
b=1
.

Denote
\Omega\in\sigma(\Xi)
the event that the preference matrix is regular defined in Corollary
4.16
. Then

\mathbb{P}(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1\,|\,c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},\Omega)\geq 1/3\,.

Probability of Liking Item Type in Terms of Regularity

We use the same notation introduced in Section
A.1
.
The next lemma expresses the posterior probability of user
u
’s preference for item
i
, conditional on the user feedback
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
obtained so far
as well as the preference matrix
\Xi
, all item types
\tau_{I}^{\sim i}
except for item
i
’s, and all user types
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
except for
u^{\prime}s
, in terms of a quantity that is controlled by the column and row regularity. We emphasize that conditioning on
\Xi,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
yields a simple expression for the posterior in terms of these quantities, but
does
not
mean giving the information to the algorithm producing recommendation
a_{u,t}
.

Lemma A.2
.

Consider a realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h
in which use
u
has not rated item
i
.
Let
\mathbf{j}_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the set of item types previously rated by user
u
and row vector
x_{u}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I}^{\sim i})
be the
\pm 1
feedback. Similarly, Let
\mathbf{j}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the set of user types that previously rated item
i
and row vector
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
be the
\pm 1
feedback.
Let
\mathbf{x}^{+}
be the vector
\mathbf{x}
appended by
+1
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\mathbf{j}_{u}|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})|}+\max_{j\in[\texttt{q}_{U}]\setminus\mathbf{j}_{u}}\frac{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{u},j)})|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}\,.

Proof.

Lemma
4.17
states that conditioning on a realization of
\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)
,
the variables
\tau_{I}(i)
and
\tau_{U}(u)
are independently uniformly distributed on
\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})
and
\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})
.
Note that user
u
likes item
i
only if
\tau_{U}(u)\in\Lambda_{+,\tau_{I}(i)}(\Xi)
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

(51)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,v}\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\tau_{U}(u)=j,\tau_{I}(i)=k,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\cdot

(52)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathbb{P}\big(\tau_{U}(u)=j,\tau_{I}(i)=k\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\Big\}

(53)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j,v}\mathds{1}\{v\in\Lambda_{+}(\Xi_{\bullet j})\}\frac{\mathds{1}\{v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}\,\,\frac{\mathds{1}\{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})\}}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})|}\,\,

(54)

To upper bound this, in terms of regularity parameters of
\Xi
, using simple algebra, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j,v}

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T}),v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\cap\Lambda_{+}(\Xi_{\bullet j})\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{j,v}\mathds{1}\{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})\cap\mathbf{j}_{u},\,\,v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\}

\displaystyle\,+\sum_{j,v}\mathds{1}\{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})\setminus\mathbf{j}_{u},\,\,v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{u},j)})\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j,v}\mathds{1}\{j\in\mathbf{j}_{u},v\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\}+|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})\setminus\mathbf{j}_{u}|\,\,\max_{j\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})\setminus\mathbf{j}_{u}}|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{u},j)})|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbf{j}_{u}|\,\,|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|+|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})|\,\,\max_{j\notin\mathbf{j}_{u}}|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{u},j)})|

where (a) uses the definition of
\Lambda

\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})\cap\Lambda_{+}(\Xi_{\bullet j})=\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{u},j)})\qquad\text{for }j\notin\mathbf{j}_{u}\,.

Plugging this into above display gives the statement of lemma.
∎

Proof of Prop.
4.2
with
b=1

We need to show the upper bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(

\displaystyle L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},\,d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},\,\Omega\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\leq 2/3\,.

(55)

where to shorten the notation, given user
u
and item
i
we defined the event
\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)
and the set
\mathsf{B_{1}}(u,i)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{c_{i}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},\,d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U},\,\Omega\Big\}

(56)

\displaystyle\mathsf{B_{1}}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{\text{realizations of }\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\text{ such that }

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)\text{ holds}\text{ and }a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t\,\big\}

(57)

be the set of possible realizations of the model parameters and history up to time
t-1
consistent with the event conditioned upon in (
55
).

Set
\mathsf{B_{1}}(u,i)
is well-defined.

We note that the condition in (
57
) is a function of the variables
\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)
, so one can determine whether the latter satisfies the former. We spell this out as follows.
The history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
determines whether item
i
has been recommended to user
u
before or not.
The value of
c_{i}^{t}
(as in Definition
3
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all users except user
u
, i.e.,
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}

6
6

6

Conditioning on the event
a_{u,t}=i
implies that
user
u
has not been recommended item
i
by time
t-1
.
Hence ambiguity in the type of user
u
does not make the value of
c_{i}^{t}
ambiguous.
.
Similarly, the value of
d_{u}^{t}
(as in Definition
4
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all items except item
i
, i.e.,
\tau_{I}^{\sim i}
.
Furthermore, the row regularity and column regularity of the preference matrix
\Xi
is a function only of
\Xi
.

Let
\mathbf{j}_{u},\mathbf{j}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{u}
and
\mathbf{x}_{i}
be as defined in Lemma
4.18
.
Note that
\mathbf{j}_{u}
is a deterministic function of realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and
\tau_{I}^{\sim i}
and
\mathbf{j}_{i}
is a deterministic function of realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
.
Given any realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and
\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\in\mathsf{B_{1}}(u,i)
,
|\mathbf{j}_{u}|=d_{u}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{U}
and
|\mathbf{j}_{i}|=c_{i}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I}
.

So for any realization of

\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})\big)\in\mathsf{B_{1}}(u,i)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\Big]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{|\mathbf{j}_{u}|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}^{T})|}+\max_{j\notin\mathbf{j}_{u}}\frac{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}^{+}}(\Xi_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{u},j)})|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{u}}(\Xi_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{u}})|}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{\mathsf{s}_{U}2^{\mathsf{s}_{I}}}{(1-\eta)\texttt{q}_{I}}+\frac{(1+\eta)\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{2^{|\mathbf{j}_{u}|+1}}}{(1-\eta)\frac{\texttt{q}_{U}}{2^{|\mathbf{j}_{u}|}}}\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{1+\eta}{1-\eta}\overset{(d)}{\leq}2/3

where (a) uses Lemma
A.2
.
(b) uses the row and column regularity of matrix
\Xi
(
\Xi^{T}\in\Omega_{\mathsf{s}_{I},\eta},\Xi\in\Omega_{\mathsf{s}_{U},\eta},
as in Definition
4.13
) and
|\mathbf{j}_{i}|<\mathsf{s}_{I}
and
|\mathbf{j}_{u}|<\mathsf{s}_{U}
. (c) uses the choice of
\mathsf{s}_{U}\leq\log\texttt{q}_{U},\mathsf{s}_{I}\leq 0.99\log\texttt{q}_{I}-5\log\log N
, and
\eta
in (
25
) and the model assumption
\texttt{q}_{I}>100\log N,\texttt{q}_{U}<n
in Section
2.2
to get

\frac{\mathsf{s}_{U}2^{\mathsf{s}_{I}}}{(1-\eta)\texttt{q}_{I}}<\frac{(\log N)\texttt{q}_{I}^{0.99}}{(1-\eta)\texttt{q}_{I}(\log N)^{5}}<\frac{2}{\texttt{q}_{I}^{0.01}(\log N)^{4}}<\frac{1}{\log^{2}N}=1/36

(d) uses
\eta=1/13
.

Applying tower property

Using the total probability lemma on above display, for any
i
such that
a_{u,s}\neq i
for all
s<t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)\big)\leq 2/3\,.

(58)

Recall that there is a random variable
\zeta_{u,t}
, independent of all other variables, such that
a_{u,t}=f_{u,t}(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\zeta_{u,t})
, for some deterministic function
f_{u,t}
.
Also, for all
i
such that
u
has not rated
i
before,
\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\Xi,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u})
. This is proved using the the same justification as above showing that the set
\mathsf{B_{1}}(u,i)
is well-defined. Clearly,
L_{u,i}\in\sigma(\Xi,\tau_{U},\tau_{I})
.
So conditioning on
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
,
\{a_{u,t}=i\}
is independent of event
\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)
and
L_{u,i}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)\big)\,.

(59)

We use these properties to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,a_{u,t})\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum_{i:a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}(u,i)\big)\overset{(c)}{\leq}2/3\,.

Here (a) uses total probability lemma
\{L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\{a_{u,t}=i,L_{u,i}=+1\}
and Eq. (
59
). (b) uses the assumption that an item can be recommended at most once to a user to take the sum only over the items never recommended to
u
before. Using Eq. (
58
) in each term of the sum gives (c).

Using the tower property over
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
on the above display gives the statement of proposition for
b=1
.
∎

A.3.2
Proof of Proposition
4.2
with
b=2

Proposition
4.2
with
b=2
.

Denote
\Omega\in\sigma(\Xi)
the event that the preference matrix is regular defined in Corollary
4.16
. Then,

\mathbb{P}(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1\,|\,c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{a_{u,t}}})^{c},\Omega)\geq 1/3\,.

The proof is very similar to the case
b=3
presented in Sec.
4.2
, switching the role of items and users. First, parallel to Lemma
4.18
, the next lemma expresses the posterior probability under
(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}})^{c}
of user
u
’s preference for item
i
conditional on the feedback
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
obtained so far as well as the preference matrix, user types
\tau_{U}
and all item types except for item
i
’s, in terms of a quantity that is controlled by the row regularity of
\Xi
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\mathbf{j}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U})
and
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{U})
be as defined in Lemma
4.17
.
Let
\mathbf{x}^{+}
be the vector
\mathbf{x}
appended by
+1
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}})^{c}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{+}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet,(\mathbf{j}_{i},\tau_{U}(u))})|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})|}\,.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{j}=\mathbf{j}_{i}
and
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{i}
.
Using
\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I})
and Lemma
4.17

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{I}(i)=j\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h,(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}})^{c}\,,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\tau_{I}(i)=j\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=h,,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{1}{\{j\in\Lambda_{x}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})\}}}{|\Lambda_{x}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|}\,.

Given the items upon which we are conditioning in the lemma statement the only uncertainty is in the type of item
i
, and moreover

L_{u,i}=+1
if and only if
\tau_{I}(i)\in\Lambda_{+}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\tau_{U}(u)})=\{j\in[\texttt{q}_{U}]:\xi_{\tau_{U}(u),j}=+1\}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}})^{c}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\Lambda_{+}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\tau_{U}(u)})\cap\Lambda_{x}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|}{|\Lambda_{x}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|}\,.

Using Definition (
5
), on the event
\big(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}}\big)^{c}
,

\tau_{U}(u)
is not among the user types that has rated item
i
, i.e.,

\tau_{U}(u)\notin\mathbf{j}
. It follows that,
|\Lambda_{+}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\tau_{U}(u)})\cap\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}})|=|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}^{+}}\big(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet(\mathbf{j},\tau_{U}(u))}\big)|
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.2
with
b=2

We need to show the upper bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\leq 2/3\,.

(60)

where to shorten the notation, given user
u
and item
i
we defined the event
\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)
and the set
\mathsf{B_{2}}(u,i)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{c_{i}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I},\,d_{u}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{U},\,\big(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),{i}}\big)^{c},\,\Omega\Big\}

(61)

\displaystyle\mathsf{B_{2}}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{\text{realizations of }\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}\big)\text{ such that }

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)\text{ holds}\text{ and }a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t\,\big\}

(62)

be the set of possible realizations of the model parameters and history up to time
t-1
consistent with the event conditioned upon in (
60
).

Set
\mathsf{B_{2}}(u,i)
is well-defined.

We note that the condition in (
29
) is a function of the variables
\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}\big)
, so one can determine whether the latter satisfies the former. We spell this out as follows.
The history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
determines whether item
i
has been recommended to user
u
before or not.
The value of
c_{i}^{t}
(as in Definition
3
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all users except user
u
, i.e.,
\tau_{U}^{\sim u}

7
7

7

Conditioning on the event
a_{u,t}=i
implies that
user
u
has not been recommended item
i
by time
t-1
.
Hence ambiguity in the type of user
u
does not make the value of
c_{i}^{t}
ambiguous.
.
Similarly, the value of
d_{u}^{t}
(as in Definition
4
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all items except item
i
, i.e.,
\tau_{I}^{\sim i}
.
Furthermore, the row regularity and column regularity of the preference matrix
\Xi
is a function only of
\Xi
.

Let
\mathbf{j}_{i}
and
\mathbf{x}_{i}
be as defined in Lemma
A.3
.
Note that
\mathbf{j}_{i}
is a deterministic function of realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and
\tau_{U}
.
Given any realization of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and
\tau_{U}\in\mathsf{B_{2}}(u,i)
, the type of item
i
is uniform on
\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}})
and
|\mathbf{j}_{i}|=c_{i}^{t}<\mathsf{s}_{I}
.

So for any realization of

\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U})\big)\in\mathsf{B_{2}}(u,i)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi,\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}\Big]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{+}}\big(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet(\mathbf{j}_{i},\tau_{U}(u))}\big)|}{|\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\big(\Xi^{T}_{\bullet\mathbf{j}_{i}}\big)|}\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{(1+\eta)2^{|\mathbf{j}_{i}|}}{(1-\eta)2^{|\mathbf{j}_{i}|+1}}\overset{(c)}{\leq}2/3\,,

where (a) uses Lemma
A.3
.
(b) uses the row regularity of matrix
\Xi
(
\Xi^{T}\in\Omega_{\mathsf{s}_{I},\eta}
as in Definition
4.13
) and
|\mathbf{j}_{i}|<\mathsf{s}_{I}
. (c) uses

\eta=1/13
.

Applying tower property

Using the total probability lemma on above display, for any
i
such that
a_{u,s}\neq i
for all
s<t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)\big)\leq 2/3\,.

(63)

Recall that there is a random variable
\zeta_{u,t}
, independent of all other variables, such that
a_{u,t}=f_{u,t}(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\zeta_{u,t})
, for some deterministic function
f_{u,t}
.
Also, for all
i
such that
u
has not rated
i
before,
\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\Xi,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U})
. This is proved using the the same justification as above showing that the set
\mathsf{B_{2}}(u,i)
is well-defined. Clearly,
L_{u,i}\in\sigma(\Xi,\tau_{U},\tau_{I})
.
So conditioning on
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
,
\{a_{u,t}=i\}
is independent of event
\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)
and
L_{u,i}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)\big)\,.

(64)

We use these properties to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,a_{u,t})\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum_{i:a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)\big)\overset{(c)}{\leq}2/3\,.

Here (a) uses total probability lemma
\{L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\{a_{u,t}=i,L_{u,i}=+1\}
and Eq. (
64
). (b) uses the assumption that an item can be recommended at most once to a user to take the sum only over the items never recommended to
u
before. Using Eq. (
63
) in each term of the sum gives (c).

Using the tower property over
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
on the above display gives the statement of proposition for
b=2
.
∎

A.3.3
Proof of Proposition
4.2
with
b=4

Proposition
4.2
with
b=4
.

\mathbb{P}(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1\,|\,c_{a_{u,t}}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I},d_{u}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{U},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({a_{u,t}})})^{c})=1/2\,.

The proof is less similar to the cases
b=1,2
and 3
3
. The major difference comes from the observation that the uncertainty in the preference of user
u
for item
i
is not due to the uncertainty in the type of either one, but rather on the value of the relevant element of preference matrix:
on event
(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})})^{c})
, even revealing the type of user
u
and the type of item
i
, the preference of
u
for
i
is uncertain. This is proved later in Lemma
A.5
whose proof requires the statement of next lemma.

Lemma A.4
.

Given
h_{t}
in which no user from type
\tau_{U}(u)
has rated any item with type
\tau_{I}(i)
by time
t-1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t},

\displaystyle\,\big|\,\xi_{v,j}=+1,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\tau_{U}(u)=v,\Xi^{\sim(v,j)},\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t},\,\big|\,\xi_{v,j}=-1,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\tau_{U}(u)=v,\Xi^{\sim(v,j)},\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)\,.

Proof.

The history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
includes all the items recommended and the associated
\pm 1
feedback up to and including time
t
; its distribution is complicated, but we do not need to calculate the posterior probability of
\mathcal{H}_{t}
in the lemma statement.

Fix
\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}
such that
\tau_{I}(i)=j
and
\tau_{U}(u)=v
. Also, fix
\Xi^{\sim(v,j)}
, as well as a specific possible trajectory for the history
h_{t}
in which no user
u^{\prime}
with type
v
has rated any item
i^{\prime}
with type
j
.

We consider two copies of the history random process
\mathcal{H}_{t}
: the first
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{1}
, where
\xi_{v,j}=+1
, and the second
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{2}
where
\xi_{v,j}=-1
.
For all users
u^{\prime}
(including
u
) and times
s\leq t
, the recommended item is described by a function
a_{u^{\prime},s}=f_{u^{\prime},s}(\mathcal{H}_{s-1},\zeta_{u^{\prime},s})
for some auxiliary independent random variable
\zeta_{u^{\prime},s}
. We thus have two copies of all these variables,
a_{u^{\prime},s}^{1}
and
a_{u^{\prime},s}^{2}
, and so forth.

We will give an inductive argument. Suppose that one of these two cases holds for some
s,0\leq s\leq t-1
:

1.

\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}=\mathcal{H}_{s}^{2}=h_{s}
, or

2.

Both
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}\neq h_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{2}\neq h_{s}
.

We will show below that at time
s+1
one of these two cases holds.
Note that the first case is tautologically true for
s=0
, since then the histories are empty, which serves as our base case. By induction, one of the two cases holds at time
t
, which implies that for any
h_{t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t},\,\big|\,\xi_{v,j}=+1,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\tau_{U}(u)=v,\Xi^{\sim(v,j)},\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t},\,\big|\,\xi_{v,j}=-1,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\tau_{U}(u)=v,\Xi^{\sim(v,j)},\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

We now prove the inductive step.
First, observe that if case 2 holds above, i.e., the histories both differ from
h_{s}
, then case 2 continues to hold for all future times.

Now suppose that case 1 holds at time
s
.
We couple the
\zeta_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{1}
and
\zeta_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{2}
random variables to be equal for all
u^{\prime}
, which results in
a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{1}=a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{2}
, i.e., all recommendations made at step
s+1
in both processes are the same. These both agree with
h_{s+1}
, or both disagree with
h_{s+1}
, and the latter case puts us into case 2 at time
s+1
.

In the former case where
a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{1}=a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{2}
agree with
h_{s+1}
, the feedback
L_{u^{\prime},a_{u^{\prime},s+1}}^{1}
and
L_{u^{\prime},a_{u^{\prime},s+1}}^{2}
from these recommendations is the same in both
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{2}
copies:

1.

For users
u^{\prime}
such that
\tau_{U}(u^{\prime})\neq v

the types and the elements of the preference matrix for these user types are the same in both copies and hence preferences are the same.

2.

For users
u^{\prime}
such that
\tau_{U}(u^{\prime})=v
, the types of users and items are the same in both copies; however, the preference of user type
v
for item type
j
is different in two copies.

However, since in the case we are analyzing,
a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{1}=a_{u^{\prime},s+1}^{2}
agree with
h_{s+1}
, they also agree with
h_{t}
.
It is also assumed that according to
h_{t}
, no user of type
v
rates any item of type
j
by time
t>s
. Hence the feedback for these users will also be the same for both copies.

This completes the argument.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Fix
\tau_{I},\tau_{U}
, and
\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))}
. Consider a copy of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
such that
(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})})^{c}\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\tau_{I},\tau_{U})
holds, i.e., according to
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
no user of type
\tau_{U}(u)
has rated any item of type
\tau_{I}(i)
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})})^{c},\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\,.

Proof.

Lemma
A.4
shows that for any
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\in(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})})^{c}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t},

\displaystyle\,\big|\,\xi_{v,j}=+1,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\tau_{U}(u)=v,\Xi^{\sim(v,j)},\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}=h_{t},\,\big|\,\xi_{v,j}=-1,\tau_{I}(i)=j,\tau_{U}(u)=v,\Xi^{\sim(v,j)},\,\tau_{I}^{\sim i},\tau_{U}^{\sim u}\big)

Hence, define
A=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})})^{c},\,\,\big|\,\xi_{\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i)}=+1,\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)
and note that
\mathbb{P}\big(\xi_{\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i)}=+1\,\big|\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)=\frac{1}{2}
, an application of Bayes theorem gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})})^{c},\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\xi_{\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i)}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})})^{c},\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{A\frac{1}{2}}{A\frac{1}{2}+2\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\,.\qed

Proof of Prop.
4.2
with
b=4
.

We need to show the upper bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,a_{u,t})\big)=1/2\,.

(65)

where to shorten the notation, given user
u
and item
i
we defined the event
\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,i)
and the set
\mathsf{B_{4}}(u,i)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{c_{i}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{I},\,d_{u}^{t}\geq\mathsf{s}_{U},\,\big(\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})}\big)^{c}\Big\}

(66)

\displaystyle\mathsf{B_{4}}(u,i)

\displaystyle=\big\{\text{realizations of }\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)\text{ such that }

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,i)\text{ holds}\text{ and }a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t\,\big\}

(67)

be the set of possible realizations of the model parameters and history up to time
t-1
consistent with the event conditioned upon in (
65
).

Set
\mathsf{B_{4}}(u,i)
is well-defined.

We note that the condition in (
67
) is a function of the variables
\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)
, so one can determine whether the latter satisfies the former. We spell this out as follows.
The history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
determines whether item
i
has been recommended to user
u
before or not.
The value of
c_{i}^{t}
(as in Definition
3
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all users.
Similarly, the value of
d_{u}^{t}
(as in Definition
4
) is a function of the previous recommendations, summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and the type of all items.
The previous recommendations summarized in
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
and the types
\tau_{I},\tau_{U}
determine whether event
\mathcal{K}^{{t}}_{\tau_{U}({u}),\tau_{I}({i})}
has occurred.

The tower property of expectation over the statement of lemma
A.5
on realization of
\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big)
in
\mathsf{B_{4}}(u,i)
gives

\mathbb{P}\big[L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\,\Xi^{\sim(\tau_{U}(u),\tau_{I}(i))},\,\tau_{I},\tau_{U}\big]=\frac{1}{2}\,.

Applying tower property

Using the total probability lemma on above display, for any
i
such that
a_{u,s}\neq i
for all
s<t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,i)\big)=1/2\,.

(68)

Recall that there is a random variable
\zeta_{u,t}
, independent of all other variables, such that
a_{u,t}=f_{u,t}(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\zeta_{u,t})
, for some deterministic function
f_{u,t}
. So conditioning on
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
,
\{a_{u,t}=i\}
is independent of event
\mathfrak{B}_{2}(u,i)
and
L_{u,i}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,i),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,i)\big)\,.

(69)

We use these properties to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,a_{u,t})\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,a_{u,t}),a_{u,t}=i\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum_{i:a_{u,s}\neq i\text{ for all }s<t}\mathbb{P}\big(a_{u,t}=i\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,a_{u,t})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,i}=+1\,\big|\,\mathcal{H}_{t-1},\mathfrak{B}_{4}(u,i)\big)\overset{(c)}{=}1/2\,.

Here (a) uses total probability lemma
\{L_{u,a_{u,t}}=+1\}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\{a_{u,t}=i,L_{u,i}=+1\}
and Eq. (
69
). (b) uses the assumption that an item can be recommended at most once to a user to take the sum only over the items never recommended to
u
before. Using Eq. (
68
) in each term of the sum gives (c).

Using the tower property over
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
on the above display gives the statement of proposition for
b=4
.
∎

A.4
Regret in terms of Bad Recommendations: Cor.
4.3

Corollary
4.3
.

The regret is lower bounded as

\mathtt{regret}(T)\geq\frac{1}{3N}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{bad}(T)\right]-\frac{1}{3N}T\,.

where using (
9
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{bad}(T)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\mathds{1}\big\{\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}\big\}\,.

Proof.

Define
B=\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}\cup\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}
.
Since
\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t},\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t},\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}
and
\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}
are disjoint events, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1,B\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1,\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}\big)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1,B\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1,B,\Omega\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1|B,\Omega\big)\mathbb{P}\big(B,\Omega\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}1/3\,\,\mathbb{P}\big(B,\Omega\big)\geq 1/3\,\big[\mathbb{P}\big(B\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\Omega^{c}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=1/3[\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{B}^{1}_{u,t}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{B}^{2}_{u,t}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{B}^{3}_{u,t}\big)]-1/3\mathbb{P}\big(\Omega^{c}\big)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1,\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}1/2\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{B}^{4}_{u,t}\big)

where in (a), and (b) we used the statement of Proposition
4.2
. Applying Corollary
4.16
which states
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{c})\leq 1/(4N)
gives

\displaystyle N\mathtt{regret}(T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\mathbb{P}\big(L_{u,a_{u,t}}=-1\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1/3\sum_{t\in[T],u\in[N]}\mathbb{P}[\cup_{b=1}^{4}\mathcal{B}^{b}_{u,t}\big)]-1/3\mathbb{P}\big(\Omega^{c}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{3}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{bad}(T)\right]-\frac{TN}{12N}\,.

∎

Appendix B
Combining Lower Bounds for Regret

B.1
Proof of Lemma
4.9

\pi_{*}
is the maximum number of users of any type as defined in Eq. (
18
)

\pi_{*}=\max_{w\in[\texttt{q}_{U}]}\big|\big\{u\in[N]:\tau_{U}(u)=w\big\}\big|\,.

A priori, the users have independent uniform types over
[\texttt{q}_{U}]
. So
\pi_{*}
is the maximum number of balls in a bin and bag argument where
N
balls are thrown into
\texttt{q}_{U}
bins. Applying Chernoff Bound, Lemma
H.1
and a union bound guarantees

\mathbb{P}(\pi_{*}>3N/\texttt{q}_{U})\leq\texttt{q}_{U}\exp(-2N/(3\texttt{q}_{U}))\leq 3/4\,,

where we used
N>20\texttt{q}_{U}\log^{2}\texttt{q}_{U}
in the last inequality.

B.2
Proof of Lemma
4.10

To prove Lemma
4.10
rigorously, we use the following definition:

Definition B.1
.

Let
r_{\textit{j}}^{T}
be the number of items with item type
j
that have been rated by time
T
:

r_{j}^{T}=|\{i:\tau_{I}(i)=j,a_{u,t}=i,u\in[N],t\leq T\}|.

For any user
u
,
T
items from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{strong}}^{T}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{weak}}^{T}
are recommended by time
T
.
The number of item types recommended to user
u
by time
T
is denoted by
\gamma_{u}^{T}=d_{u}^{T+1}
. So
T\leq\gamma_{u}^{T}\max_{j}r_{j}^{T}
for all
u
and

\max_{j}r_{j}^{T}\geq\frac{T}{\min_{u}\gamma_{u}^{T}}=\frac{T}{\gamma_{*}^{T}}\,.

(70)

Lemma
4.10
is a direct consequence of the above property and the following lemma.
8
8

8

A tighter bound in the statement of Lemma
4.10
can be constructed as follows.
Lemma
B.2
shows that with probability at least half,
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}>\Theta_{\texttt{q}_{I}}\left(\frac{T}{\gamma_{*}^{T}}\right)
where the function
\Theta_{q}(k)
is defined as

\displaystyle\Theta_{q}(k):=\begin{cases}1\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,k\leq 2\\
\sqrt{q}/3\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,2<k\leq\frac{3}{\log 2}\log q\\
q/2\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,\frac{3}{\log 2}\log q<k\leq 8\log q\\
kq/4\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,8\log q<k\,.\end{cases}

But this tighter bound results in more complex description of statement of Therorem
4.12
and the improvement in the statement of the Theorem is over the multiplicative logarithmic terms. Hence, we decided to use the form in Equation (
22
).

Lemma B.2
.

For
r_{j}^{T}
in Definition (
B.1
), with probability at least half,

\displaystyle\max_{\textit{j}\in[\texttt{q}_{I}]}r_{j}^{T}<\begin{cases}2\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}<k_{0}:=\sqrt{\texttt{q}_{I}}/3\\
3\,\frac{\log q}{\log\texttt{q}_{I}-\log I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}}\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,k_{0}\leq I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}<k_{1}:=\texttt{q}_{I}/2\\
8\log\texttt{q}_{I}\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,k_{1}\leq I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}<k_{2}:=2\texttt{q}_{I}\log\texttt{q}_{I}\\
{4I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}}/{\texttt{q}_{I}}\,,\,\quad&\text{if }\,k_{2}\leq I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}\,.\end{cases}

The proof of this lemma is exactly the same as the proof of Claim 7.9. in
[
RS1
]
.
We repeat the proof here for the sake of completeness

Proof.

First, we define a useful martinagle.
Let
r^{t}=(r_{1}^{t},\dots,r_{\texttt{q}_{I}}^{t})
where
r_{\textit{j}}^{t}
is defined in (
B.1
). Note that
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{t}=\sum_{\textit{j}\in[\texttt{q}_{I}]}r_{\textit{j}}^{t}
is the total number of recommended items at the end of time
t
. Any new item has type uniformly distributed on
[\texttt{q}_{I}]
; as a consequence, the sequence
r_{j}^{t}-I_{\mathsf{total}}^{t}/\texttt{q}_{I}
is a martingale with respect to filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma(r^{0},r^{1},\dots,r^{t})
, because
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{t}
is incremented whenever a new item is recommended and each new item increases
r^{t}_{j}
by one with probability
1/\texttt{q}_{I}
.

It turns out to be easier to work with a different martingale that considers recommendations to each user separately, so that the item counts are incremented by at most one at each step. Consider the lexicographical ordering on pairs
(t,u)
, where
(s,v)\leq(t,u)
if either
s<t
or
s=t
and
v\leq u
(such that the recommendation to user
v
at time
s
occurred before that of user
u
at time
t
). For
j\in[\texttt{q}_{I}]
, let

r_{j}^{t,u}=\Big|\big\{i:\tau_{I}(i)=j,a_{v,s}=i\text{ for some }(s,v)\leq(t,u)\big\}\Big|\,.

Let
r^{t,u}=(r_{1}^{t,u},\dots,r_{\texttt{q}_{I}}^{t,u})
and define
\rho^{t,u}=\sum_{j}r_{j}^{t,u}
to be the total number of items recommended by
(t,u)
,
e.g.
,
\rho^{T,N}=I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
.
We now define a sequence of stopping times
Z_{k}\in\mathbb{N}\times[N]
,

Z_{k}=\min\big\{(t,u)>Z_{k-1}:\rho^{t,u}>\rho^{t,u-1}\big\}\,,

where
(t,0)
is interpreted as
(t-1,N)
and
Z_{0}=(0,N)
.
Z_{k}
is the first
(t,u)
such that a new item is recommended by the algorithm for the
k
-th time, so
\rho^{Z_{k}}=k
. The
Z_{k}
are stopping times with respect to
(\rho^{t,u})
, and observe that
k^{*}=\max\{k:Z_{k}\leq(T,N)\}=\rho^{T,N}=I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
since
I_{\mathsf{total}}^{T}
